1. PRODUCT SUMMARY


Working title: High & Undead
Alternate titles from drafts: Blood, Lust & Leftovers; Bitten by Desire; Zombified: Mind Over Mind; Natural Survival. Select one official title before public-facing work. The rest are parked alternatives, not simultaneous titles.


Format: Browser-first 2D survival-crafting sandbox with narrative RPG, stealth, exploration, and simulation elements. Single-player is the first-release target. A semi-open world of connected authored regions is preferred over an infinite world.


Premise: The player controls Phoenix, a newly turned zombie who retains a continuous inner life. Humans fear or hunt her; ordinary zombies may detect her as different. She must survive by scavenging, crafting, managing bloodlust, using two opposed disguises, and uncovering the ChroniX outbreak. Her actions shape whether she seeks restoration, accepts an undead identity, builds coexistence, or walks away from factions.


Player promise: Experience survival from the perspective of a thinking zombie—not a human fighting a zombie horde. The signature challenge is to pass among both humans and undead while balancing instinct, memory, and chosen identity.


Audience: Adult players interested in survival-crafting, atmospheric horror, character-driven choices, dark humor, and optional mature themes. Content ratings, age gates, and storefront rules must be confirmed per target platform before release.


Tone: Body horror, dark observational humor, melancholy, tenderness, and hard-won clarity. The humor should not trivialize harm or turn the whole apocalypse into a joke.


2. DESIGN PILLARS


1. The zombie is the protagonist. Her body and instincts are not cosmetic variations on a human-survivor game.
2. Survival is about concealment and control as much as combat. Passing as human and passing as undead are separate, conflicting goals.
3. Systems express identity. Feeding, blood mixtures, mutation, cannabis use, relationships, and recovered memories carry different mechanical and social consequences.
4. Crafting is legible and materially grounded. The Multi-Stick embodies the early-game, multi-use tool philosophy.
5. Story is discovered in fragments. Locations, records, objects, voices, and former coworkers advance both the mystery and present-day options.
6. Actions have persistent consequences without a single moral-ranking meter. Track relationships, factions, local state, and story flags separately.
7. The world is reactive but authored where it matters. Procedural placement and encounters add variation; major characters, story reveals, and critical clues remain authored or guaranteed.
8. Scope is earned through prototypes. A working, understandable slice outranks a huge list of unfinished systems.


3. CORE GAME LOOP


Explore and read threats → scavenge materials, clues, and blood → choose a route or disguise → craft, cultivate, or prepare → survive through stealth, combat, flight, or negotiation → rest and maintain relationships → recover memories and story options → influence local faction and ecological state → repeat.


Moment-to-moment tension comes from limited carry capacity, time exposed, bloodlust, disguise degradation, noise, scent, weather, and the chance that a fight attracts additional threats. Quiet stretches support shelter improvement, cultivation, conversation, planning, and memory discoveries.


4. STORY AND CHARACTER


4.1 Phoenix


Phoenix’s connection to ChroniX and the Euphoria project is the fixed narrative backbone. The player may choose a name, appearance, background emphasis, selected memories, and personality leanings, but the character creator should not erase the central outbreak history. Starting-background effects on skills, dialogue, initial location, and clue order remain to be designed.


Phoenix wakes amid a newly unfolding outbreak. At first she believes she is living an ordinary morning. Physical wrongness, contradictory emergency messages, a familiar badge or work object, and the reaction of humans and zombies progressively reveal what she has become. She realizes she is still thinking, then loses consciousness under the shock and hunger. On waking, she begins to survive and investigate.


Emotional arc:
- Opening: shock, confusion, immediate survival.
- Early story: test the body, manage bloodlust, find shelter, meet people who challenge her self-image.
- Middle story: investigate ChroniX, recover memories, learn to use undead abilities, decide what power costs.
- Late story: choose how to treat the research and whether to seek restoration, embrace undead life, mediate, lead, or remain independent.


The design should not declare that high “humanity” is virtuous and low humanity is evil. Track concrete behavior and relationship history; let the ending reflect the player's pattern of choices.


4.2 Opening sequence and first playable


Preferred sequence: a familiar morning routine and apartment exploration establish ordinary life; subtle sensory anomalies and interrupted messages raise unease; the street reveals the riot and outbreak; a zombie ignores Phoenix while a human recoils; Phoenix recognizes that she is undead but still forming coherent thoughts; panic and hunger overwhelm her; the first controllable survival scene begins in a nearby alley or safe interior.


A competing draft starts Phoenix on a lab table with a Subject 17 toe tag, a corridor escape, then a chaotic street. These openings cannot both be the definitive first scene. Recommended reconciliation: use the apartment morning as the authored opening, then reveal the lab-table event through fragmented memory. If a faster horror-first opening is preferred, the lab sequence can instead be the opening and the apartment becomes a memory. Decide before scripting production scenes.


First playable slice should teach, in context: movement and interaction, inspection, stealth/noise, a first bloodlust spike, one cannabis use, scavenging, the Multi-Stick, a first disguise choice, one enemy response, and a safe place. The slice must not require harming a human or consuming explicit content to progress.


4.3 Memory delivery


Memory triggers include ChroniX locations, photographs, emails, terminals, recordings, familiar voices, coworkers, personal effects, Euphoria research, and dreams. Each memory should add a present-use benefit: dialogue, a quest lead, journal entry, recipe, ability, relationship context, or verified clue. Keep core story-critical clues authored or guaranteed; randomness may vary where and how optional clues are found, never whether the player can understand the main plot.


Four inner perspectives are proposed: Scientist, Survivor, Hungry One, and Witness. Treat them as expressive internal voices rather than separate characters or a diagnosis. Their prominence can respond to state and choices. Their mechanical effects need clear, testable definitions before implementation.


4.4 Acts and major choice


Act I — Awareness and survival: awaken, escape immediate danger, secure a provisional shelter, establish the first survival loop, meet key survivors, hear the first signal, and choose to approach ChroniX.


Act II — Investigation and power: enter the Industrial Zone or Deep Forest route; investigate the ChroniX campus; meet Dr. Lena Voss and Riven; learn about other conscious subjects; gain access to advanced cultivation and Lab-Grade samples; face competing human and undead demands. A central research-spire set piece should support stealth, combat, blood abilities, and social solutions.


Act II climax choice, proposed: destroy the data and samples; preserve them for cure research; or use them to pursue a major personal evolution. This choice should matter but not be the sole determinant of the ending.


Act III — Identity and consequence: resolve relationships, faction conflicts, the fate of the research, and Phoenix’s future through accumulated story flags. Endings should recognize patterns of play rather than score morality.


Possible ending directions to consolidate: full or partial restoration; human–undead treaty or coexistence; embracing undead identity/evolution; destroying ChroniX or controlling its research; independent survival; ecological restoration; a quiet garden/community ending. Several later drafts add ruler, extinction, migration, collective-governance, and time-loop outcomes. Defer those until the core ending set is authored and reachable.


4.5 Key cast and conscious subjects


Core cast proposed:
- Marcus Reyes: former EMT and human-trust test. Values observable reliability and protection of people in his care. His support should depend on witnessed actions, not only dialogue points.
- Dr. Lena Voss: researcher connected to ChroniX and possible treatment. Her guilt, knowledge, and motives should unfold gradually.
- Riven (Subject 18): conscious undead who challenges Phoenix to accept undead identity. Mentor, ideological foil, ally, rival, or optional romance route if the adult relationship design is retained in non-explicit form.
- Dr. Elias Thorne / ChroniX Inner Circle: central opposing force seeking research control, outbreak leverage, a cure or weapon, and unusual infected subjects. Thorne's personal motive is intentionally unresolved and should be revealed as a mystery.
- Iris (Subject 15): botanist and grower; potential cannabis and ecological ally.
- Harlan Crowe (Subject 14): former security figure who favors rules and self-governance among conscious undead.
- Marrow (Subject 06): predatory conscious undead and ideological antagonist.
- Dr. Sofia Nguyen (Subject 19): hidden researcher pursuing a cure, subject to narrative consolidation.
- Bell (Subject 21): broadcaster preserving names, records, music, and messages.


Other proposed subjects: 03 Dr. Elias Kane; 05 Static; 08 an unstable/fragmentary subject; 09 Lila Vey; 11 Rack; 12 a contained, minimally verbal subject; 13 Kestrel; 16 missing; plus the characters above. Source drafts disagree on subject counts: one calls them “at least eleven,” another lists eleven, and a later roster lists fourteen other subjects. Use the fourteen-name roster only as a candidate cast list; do not state the count as settled canon until the chronology and numbering are reconciled. Kane's death and Thorne's role also need consistency checks.


5. WORLD, REGIONS, AND FACTIONS


5.1 World structure


Use a semi-open network of distinct regions connected by walkable routes, interiors, trails, waterways, and transitions. Chunk streaming is a technical target, not a claim of seamless implementation. Keep story gates for major progression while allowing ordinary travel to feel connected. Start with one compact region and expand only after proving performance and the exploration loop.


Proposed regions: City Center / urban ruins; Suburban Fringe; Industrial Zone; Coastal Strip; Deep Forest; Farmlands; Mountain Ridge; Swamp; Lab Complex. Military outposts, desert areas, tunnels, rivers, caves, and additional wilderness are optional extensions. Resolve overlap between “Forest,” “Deep Forest,” and “Suburban Forest” in a final map.


Region identities should have distinct resources, threats, routes, weather, and story opportunities. Urban ruins: dense salvage and patrols. Forest: concealment, wildlife, plants, and timber. Coast and waterways: fish, float materials, tides, and storms. Farms: cultivation and animal resources, exposed to weather and raids. Industrial areas: scrap, machinery, contamination, and noise. Lab: research, samples, alarms, and story-critical clues.


5.2 Points of interest


Candidate POIs: apartment and alley; School Gym survivor camp; Ranger Cabin; Water Tower Safehouse; Subway Control Room; Marina and Tidal Caves; Rail Yard; Tunnel Network; Quarantined Hospital; Collapsed Metro; abandoned grow site; Silo Complex; Iris’s Trading Post; Marrow’s gathering ground; Mountain Relay Station; Kestrel’s lookout; Subject 12’s room; ChroniX Greenhouse Wing; central Spire; Kane’s office; Lila’s apartment; river ford; overpass; Old Clinic. Assign every POI one clear region, purpose, access condition, risk, and reward before content production.


5.3 Factions


Core faction perspectives:
- ChroniX Inner Circle: control research and outbreak; capture unusual infected subjects.
- Resistance / Free Settlements: protect survivors and rebuild.
- Scavengers: control resources and territory through pragmatism.
- Cultists: interpret infection as evolution or transformation.
- Lone Wolves: independence and minimal obligations.
- Conscious Undead Network: internal politics between restraint, coexistence, and predation.


Later faction drafts add Remnant Authority, Predators, Green Circle, Lazarus Loyalists, Free Settlements, and named leadership. These may be refinements of the groups above, not automatically additional factions. Consolidate into a small launch roster with distinct beliefs and playable consequences.


Track faction reputation separately from individual NPC trust and affinity. Territory, stability, influence, unity, and war readiness are possible advanced variables; begin with reputation, local presence, and a small set of authored event conditions. Faction collapse should create a readable vacuum that may be filled by another faction, threats, or a community.


6. SURVIVAL AND IDENTITY SYSTEMS


6.1 Core meters


Recommended launch set: Health, Stamina, Bloodlust, Decay, and a single Mental Clarity/Consciousness variable. Hydration and temperature can be added if they materially support the chosen prototype. Fatigue should be distinct from Stamina only if it has a separate timescale and decisions. Do not ship multiple overlapping meters called Energy, Consciousness, Mental Stability, Sanity, Fatigue, Humanity, and Memory without precise definitions.


- Health: damage and defeat state.
- Stamina: short-term exertion for movement and combat.
- Bloodlust: rising urge and control pressure; increases with time, nearby humans/wounds, combat, and other tuned conditions. Feeding reduces it but may carry ethical, social, and disguise costs.
- Decay: changes appearance and may affect movement, health, disguise, and social responses. Distinguish temporary surface condition from deeper irreversible progression only if the UI explains both.
- Mental clarity/consciousness: narrative and cognitive capacity, not a morality score; memory recovery and player actions may change dialogue and internal voices.


State thresholds and rates remain to be balanced. One draft gives Calm/Hungry/Uncontrolled bands of 0–39, 40–74, and 75–100; another begins hunger damage at 90+. Treat those as prototype candidates, not final math. The UI must show consequences and warnings clearly.


6.2 Blood alchemy and mutations


Blood sources proposed: human, zombie/walker, Brute/Spitter/Lab-grade, predator (wolf/bear/boar), prey (deer/rabbit/sheep), and Phoenix’s own. Fish, rat, dog, cat, and other animal types are additional candidate sources. Exact availability and ethical consequences depend on source and location.


Core proposal: samples have source, freshness, quality, volume, potency, and possible mutation resonance. Fresh samples are stronger and spoil sooner; stale samples are weaker but more stable. Mix up to three blood types at an appropriate bench, with a simpler two-component field mix if retained. A stabilizer slot for resin or rare lab chemicals is an optional advanced rule.


Effects may alter stamina, strength, regeneration, perception, night vision, speed, decay resistance, disguise, or combat behavior. Drawbacks may include instability, increased bloodlust, tolerance, reduced disguise, temporary weakness, or visible mutations. Repeated use can contribute toward permanent mutations. Mutations should change appearance, options, and NPC reactions; they are identity choices, not merely numeric upgrades.


Open decisions: reconcile mutation slots (starting at one, proposed maximum four, plus a Master perk slot and New Game+ bonus); define respec/removal rules; decide whether resonance is visible, partially hinted, or hidden; define mixing formula, spoilage timing, trade legality, and testable balance values. Avoid hidden outcomes that materially affect a player choice.


6.3 Cannabis and hemp


Cannabis is a fictionalized gameplay resource woven through survival, cultivation, crafting, trade, and setting. Proposed effects: temporary bloodlust suppression, possible changes to clarity or perception, and selected fictional effects on decay. Hemp supplies fiber and materials for rope, cordage, fabric, clothing, bags, fishing line, nets, insulation, and shelter components. Avoid claiming every product is medically effective outside the fictional game.


Cycle: find or trade seeds; prepare a plot; plant and tend; grow through visible stages; harvest; dry/cure; process into smokeables, edibles, extracts, resin, oil, or hemp fiber; record results in a journal. Strains can differ in fictional effects, quality, growth needs, rarity, and tolerance. Rain, heat, storms, cold, light, water, and protection may affect crop yield or risk.


A prototype effect in the source gives a specific reduction and suppression period; other drafts propose different effects and rates. Select a single MVP effect and communicate its exact result in UI. Exact recipes, quantities, strain statistics, tolerance, and decay effects remain open. Cannabis should be a meaningful option, not the sole way to control bloodlust or an obligatory grind. Include a challenge run that can disable it.


6.4 Humanity, consciousness, memory


A Humanity track appears in later drafts, while earlier guidance rejects one moral-ranking meter. Prefer a multi-variable record: remembered events, relationships, chosen coping strategies, feeding history, promises, and faction actions. If a numeric identity variable is used, it must not equate “human” with “good” or lock all routes unfairly. Memory is a collectible narrative system: fragments can unlock known recipes, abilities, dialogue, quests, and endings, but random generation must not block the main plot.


7. STEALTH, DETECTION, AND WORLD AI


7.1 Dual disguise


Humans and zombies use distinct detection profiles. Humans evaluate sight, posture, speech, movement, wounds, clothing, blood, noise, light, and suspicious behavior. Zombies emphasize scent, sound, gait, blood, and group behavior. Animals add species-specific scent, fear, and hearing.


Possible counterplay: clean clothes and plausible movement against humans; rotting matter, zombie blood, and mimicked gait against zombies; cover and line-of-sight breaks; crouching; quiet surfaces; scent masking; distractions; hiding; route planning; controlled exposure. One disguise must not automatically fool both populations. Rain can wash external disguise away; fog reduces sight; weather may shift sound and scent propagation.


Use readable states: unaware → suspicious → investigating → searching → chasing/attacking. Show what channel caused suspicion when feasible. Avoid “invisible” timers with no feedback. Scent trails and deliberate scent lures are advanced options, not MVP dependencies.


7.2 Enemy and NPC behavior


Candidate enemy roles: Walkers, Runners, Brutes, Spitters, Lab Mutants, Screamer, feral conscious pack, human scavenger, organized patrol/lookout, guard dogs, and wildlife. Their attack kits and tactical roles need authored, readable definitions. Difficulty should vary enemy composition and behavior, not merely add health or bodies.


Humans may patrol, loot, trade, build, flee, investigate sound, set traps, recruit, defend settlements, and react to reputation. Zombies may wander, follow scent and sound, roam in groups, feed, return to patrol, or become more active at night. NPCs can remember a small number of significant witnessed actions rather than simulating unlimited memory.


Procedural encounters should be region- and progression-aware, fair, and legible. Keep key character introductions and story events authored. Local world simulation may use detailed AI near the player and abstract periodic updates at distance; exact actor caps and update intervals must be established through profiling.


8. COMBAT, INJURY, AND ABILITIES


Combat is dangerous, relatively brief, noisy, and optional where the level supports stealth or escape. Phoenix begins with claws/scratches, bite/grab, shove/throw, and brute strength; later options can include kicks, punches, improvised weapons, spears, bows, thrown objects, and mutation abilities. Firearms and crossbows are not core assumptions; if added, scarcity and noise should preserve the stealth trade-off.


Bloodlust states can alter control and capability: Calm preserves deliberate defense and stealth; Hungry can improve aggression or strength while narrowing social options; Uncontrolled risks attacking allies and exposing Phoenix. Uncontrolled should provide warnings and ways to recover, not cause arbitrary unavoidable relationship loss.


Combat features under consideration: attack tells, stamina and cooldowns, hit reactions, stagger, knockback, enemy health states, limb damage, noise propagation, weapon durability, feeding as a vulnerable action, environmental hazards, and companion support. Detailed body-part simulation and dismemberment are advanced scope; do not place them in the first playable slice unless essential to proving the game.


Abilities can develop through skill use, quests, discoveries, crafting, and mutations: tracking senses, climbing, stealth movement, mobility, resilience, and bloodlust control. Avoid uncontrolled power creep; every strong mutation should have a legible trade-off.


9. GATHERING, INVENTORY, CRAFTING, AND THE MULTI-STICK


9.1 Resources


Candidate resources: branches, logs, bark, wood, stone, flint, clay, mud, sand, metal scrap, plastic, fabric, grass, moss, plant fiber, hemp, seeds, cannabis, berries, apples, water, shells, salt, bones, hides, feathers, bottles, containers, and blood. Resource availability and tool requirements should be assigned by region and tested for scarcity without grind.


Gathering can be by hand, tool-assisted, or scavenging containers and sites. Yield may vary by biome, weather, time, tool quality, and skill. Actions take time and can generate noise or exposure. Digging is a player-initiated search action at suitable ground, with authored caches and low-value/no-find outcomes; random digging must not replace meaningful POI exploration.


9.2 Multi-Stick


The Multi-Stick is the signature early tool and should remain useful rather than becoming disposable. Candidate functions: walking aid, striking, prying, reaching, digging, fishing spear, plant/grass cutting, trap anchor, carrying pole, tent/raft support, leverage, and later combat techniques. Not every use needs to be available in the first build.


Proposed progression: raw branch → sharpened stick → fire-hardened → bound/weighted → stone-tipped → bone-reinforced → metal/composite variants. High-tech and “legendary” tiers are optional late-game ideas. The source explicitly identifies one early recipe: one branch plus one stone for a sharpened Multi-Stick; other quantities need design. The item should be repairable or reshapeable to a basic branch so it is not permanently lost.


9.3 Crafting and repair


Simple known recipes can be made in the field; advanced recipes require suitable stations. Recipes should display ingredients, time, output, durability, skill/station requirements, discovery state, and any trade-offs before crafting. Consume ingredients only on a successful craft. Discovery sources include exploration, experiment, memory, NPCs, and research.


Candidate crafts: stone knife, axe/hatchet, hammer, spear, bow and arrows, traps, rope, backpack, clothing, shoes, water container/filter, fire pit, pipe, storage, shelter parts, field mixer, extraction kit, rafts, and bicycle/carts. Exact quantities, times, durability, and workbench tiers remain open. Field repair can be partial; workbench repair can be more complete. Quality tiers are a proposal and should not require Legendary tiers for a satisfying core loop.


9.4 Inventory and economy


Use weight plus a soft slot/capacity limit, with clear encumbrance thresholds and quick-drop/storage actions. Backpacks, hauler companions, storage, and selected abilities can expand capacity. Item stacks, freshness, spoilage, durability, equipment slots, categories, and quest-item safety need rules.


Use barter and reputation as the leading economy proposal. Human markets may value water, medicine, tools, information, and useful supplies. Conscious-undead markets may value blood, extracts, mutation knowledge, and rare recipes. Avoid universal currency unless later testing supports it. Do not add paid survival resources, pay-to-win boosts, or monetized core systems.


10. SHELTERS, ECOLOGY, AND TRANSPORT


Shelter progression candidates: lean-to/tarp, tent, hut/shed, treehouse, converted cave, underground shelter, and upgraded safehouses. Stations may include storage, workbench, grow bed, drying rack, animal pen, water collector, fire pit, blood bench, and vehicle workshop. Comfort and security can improve rest and crafting; Security Level 2 permanence is a proposal that requires clear costs and rules.


World persistence should focus on player-relevant local state: claimed safehouses, crops, looted caches, faction presence, and significant ecological changes. Example rule to test: cleared areas become temporarily safer, then repopulate or attract new groups; looted stashes remain depleted for a defined interval. Exact respawn and off-screen simulation rules remain open.


Transportation candidates: logs for temporary flotation, rafts, bicycles, cargo carts/trailers, motorized bicycles or scooters, and animal hauling/riding. Water crossings and routes can open exploration options. Vehicle inventories, repair, fuel, and recipes are later scope.


Animal candidates include rat, rabbit, cat, dog, coyote, wolf, deer, boar, bear, horse, mule, cow, sheep, goat, and birds. Start with a small roster. Taming can use approach, suitable offerings, repeated trust-building, temperament, bloodlust/disguise state, and shelter. Roles may include Scout, Guard, Hauler, Companion, Mount, or Producer. Training, breeding, death, recovery, and replacement rules remain open; permanent companion loss must be signaled and designed with care.


11. NPC RELATIONSHIPS, QUESTS, AND ENDINGS


Track individual trust and relationship history separately from faction reputation. Characters may react to keeping promises, witnessed feeding, protecting people, losing control, helping rival groups, and research decisions. Trust can unlock conversation, quests, support, trade, or access; affinity is optional and character-specific.


Mature relationship approach: all romance-capable characters are consenting adults; romance and intimacy are optional, non-explicit in the baseline document, skippable, and never required for progression or endings. Provide global content settings and per-scene skip where appropriate. Preserve friendship, loyalty, and companionship as meaningful paths. Intimacy should not be the primary or most powerful survival-meter consumable; avoid making relationships a resource exploit.


Quest categories: main mystery, character/faction side quests, exploration discoveries, and systemic events. Record major decisions as story flags. Keep main quests available where possible; optional quests may change as the world changes, with clear warnings before irreversible closure. The journal records current goals, recovered clues, memory fragments, and map knowledge.


Endings should depend on accumulated identity choices, relationships, faction support, bloodlust management, mutation, and research handling. Difficulty and accessibility settings must not lock story endings. Consolidate all proposed ending names and requirements into one reachable ending matrix after the core story is stable.


12. CHARACTER CREATION AND MATURE CONTENT BOUNDARIES


Player customization candidates: name, face, skin tone and zombie coloration, hair, eyes, scars, tattoos, clothing, initial decay appearance, selected background, starting skill emphasis, and personality leanings. Keep cosmetic customization separate from gameplay-affecting background, clothing, and mutation effects. Pronouns, voice selection, body proportions, and changing appearance after start require scope decisions.


The source includes explicit nudity and sexual-content requests. This consolidated baseline limits design support to optional, non-explicit adult romance, consent, boundaries, and mature themes. Keep the character creator focused on non-explicit appearance, avoid sexualized presentation of uncertain-age characters, and ensure all romanceable characters are clearly adults. Any platform-specific age verification and content requirements need legal/storefront review before release.


13. AUDIO, VISUALS, AND INTERFACE


Visual direction candidates conflict: pixel art/top-down, 2D stylized sprites, 2.5D, and 3D promotional website mockups all appear. For the browser MVP, choose 2D top-down or 2D side-on before asset work; top-down currently best matches the open-world scavenging and stealth loop. The visual style should contrast ruined urban spaces with returning vegetation, and use mutations and decay as visible storytelling.


Audio should communicate distance, weather, detection, pursuit, state changes, and memory. A four-state adaptive score—calm, tension, combat/chase, uncontrolled horror—is a later production target. Phoenix’s voice and inner voices can change with condition, with captions and visual equivalents for critical cues.


HUD: keep Bloodlust, Decay, disguise condition, and quick-use items prominent; show Stamina and detection/noise cues contextually. Avoid an overloaded HUD of every proposed meter. Menus: inventory/crafting, map, journal, character/identity, relationships, cultivation journal, and blood mixing as systems enter scope. Communicate suspicious-state causes and recipe effects clearly.


Accessibility candidates: remappable controls, text scaling, colorblind-safe indicators, screen-reader support where feasible, captions for key audio cues, reduced flashing/gore, combat assists, adjustable detection/survival rates, and clear tutorial prompts. Test whether all essential information works without audio.


14. TECHNICAL DIRECTION AND AI USE


The technical drafts disagree between Godot, Unity, Babylon.js, Three.js, and pure Canvas/JavaScript. For a browser-first 2D game, choose one implementation path after a small rendering/input prototype. Keep the simulation data-driven and deterministic where practical. A finite connected-region world is recommended before infinite streaming.


Suggested architecture boundaries: input and player controller; world/time/weather; entities and perception; survival meters; inventory/crafting; dialogue/quests/flags; faction/local simulation; save/load; UI. Store recipes, items, strain data, dialogue, and encounter tables as data, not scattered hard-coded rules. Use a saved world seed for repeatable procedural variation. Keep saves versioned and resilient.


AI should primarily mean game-agent behavior and procedural variation, not dependence on an external generative service. Use authored content for the main plot, named characters, and critical clues. Procedural systems may vary minor encounters, patrols, loot placement, resource locations, and optional events under validation and content rules. Do not generate unsafe or incoherent narrative content dynamically. AI-generated player-facing text, portraits, voices, or online services would require moderation, licensing, privacy, and cost decisions.


The source also contains a separate PotGrowHub content/SEO and AR/VR commerce idea, several website design briefs, and pasted React application code. These are not game systems and are excluded from this GDD; track them as a separate product/project if they are still wanted. The pasted prototype snippets are illustrative and internally incomplete; they are not a verified working codebase.


15. PLATFORM, MONETIZATION, AND RELEASE


The user's stated target is a playable browser game with revenue potential. Treat browser-first as the priority; Windows and mobile/Chromebook compatibility are later targets, not guaranteed parity. Make a small offline-capable single-player prototype before adding accounts, servers, cloud saves, or multiplayer.


Potential monetization consistent with the notes: paid base game or optional cosmetic content and substantial expansions after a complete base-game arc. Do not use pay-to-win, purchasable survival resources, or paid access to core survival systems. Browser advertising, subscriptions, memberships, supporter benefits, adult-content distribution, payments, and age checks need separate business and platform validation. Do not promise particular storefronts or monetization permissions until checked.


No multiplayer for MVP. Co-op, persistent online worlds, AI chat, mod marketplaces, seasonal events, and AR/VR are future possibilities only; they conflict with the stated browser 2D scope or add substantial production, safety, and moderation costs.


16. PRIORITIZED BACKLOG


MVP / first vertical slice:
- One small authored neighborhood with apartment or lab opening, alley/street, and one safe interior.
- One movement scheme, interaction, simple collision, and clear camera.
- Phoenix with Health, Stamina, Bloodlust, and basic Decay feedback.
- One zombie archetype and one human encounter; simple sight/sound/scent checks with readable warnings.
- Scavenging for branch, stone, cloth, cannabis item, and one blood sample.
- Craft the Multi-Stick and demonstrate at least three functions.
- One temporary disguise decision that helps one population but compromises the other.
- One cannabis consumable with a clear, testable bloodlust effect; no required cultivation timer in this first slice.
- One short memory/story clue that changes a present objective or dialogue option.
- Basic inventory, save/load, and accessibility settings for remapping, text scale, and captions or visual cues.
- A short authored objective chain that can be completed without external instructions.


P1 — prove the survival loop:
- Shelter claim and rest; simple inventory capacity and durability.
- One cultivation cycle with a single strain; one workbench and a few data-driven recipes.
- Two or three distinct enemy roles; improved stealth and sound cues.
- Marcus trust and one authored quest; one faction reputation track.
- One adjacent region with persistent local changes and a deterministic seed.


P2 — narrative and systemic depth:
- Lena, Riven, ChroniX campus, authored Act I/II quest chain.
- Blood mixing and first mutations with visible trade-offs.
- More strains, recipes, NPC memory events, barter, weather, and small-animal companion.
- Additional regions, safehouses, rafts/bicycle as scope allows.


P3 — full game candidates:
- Act III and consolidated endings, broader faction simulation, more animals, advanced base building, additional transport, extensive audio and accessibility polish, New Game+, modding.
- Co-op or persistent multiplayer only after the single-player game succeeds and architecture/business needs are separately validated.


17. FIRST PROTOTYPE ACCEPTANCE CHECKS


- A new player understands that Phoenix is a conscious zombie through play rather than a long exposition dump.
- A player can move, scavenge, craft the Multi-Stick, suppress or manage bloodlust, and choose stealth or flight in one short session.
- Human and zombie detection behave differently and communicate why suspicion rises.
- Every displayed recipe/effect matches the result applied in the game.
- A failed or missed optional action cannot soft-lock the main objective.
- Save and reload restore the current scene, player state, inventory, time, and story flags consistently.
- Playtesters can explain the next objective and the primary trade-off of their disguise choice.
- Performance is measured in real target browsers before expanding world size or actor count.


18. OPEN DECISIONS / CONFLICT REGISTER


Resolve these before treating the design as production canon:
1. Official title and brand; choose one among High & Undead and earlier alternatives.
2. Opening: apartment-morning realization versus lab-table-first cold open; recommended resolution is apartment first, lab as memory.
3. Protagonist canonical name: Phoenix Blaze appears in later material; earlier versions often use Phoenix only. Confirm whether Blaze is canon.
4. Corporation and project names: ChroniX/Euphoria are the strongest repeated story notes; Meridian Bioviral, LAZARUS-VERDE, and other project names appear in later drafts. Choose one coherent set, or explicitly make one a rebrand/cover name.
5. Antagonist: Dr. Elias Thorne is the earlier main antagonist; later subject rosters include Dr. Elias Kane as deceased ethicist. Preserve their distinction and reconcile naming to avoid accidental duplicate “Elias” confusion.
6. Subject list and number: reconcile “at least eleven,” eleven listed, and fourteen listed; establish a canonical roster and subject numbering.
7. Genre/camera/art: commit to 2D top-down or side-on for first release; defer 3D, VR, and webpage aesthetics.
8. Engine/platform: choose a browser implementation and validate export/performance before promising PC, mobile, iOS, Chromebook, or offline parity.
9. Meter model: define Bloodlust, Decay, Health, Stamina, Mental Clarity, and Fatigue distinctly; remove redundant meters from the MVP.
10. Blood system: source taxonomy, collection rules, freshness, mix formula, stabilizer, duration, tolerance, mutation slots, respec, and preview disclosure.
11. Cannabis system: first strain and effect, cultivation timescale, quality, recipes, trade, tolerance, and relationship to decay. Decide whether consumption is a reliable option or essential dependency; avoid a single mandatory coping item.
12. Humanity and consciousness: separate narrative identity from morality; decide whether either is numeric or inferred from event history.
13. Factions: merge overlapping groups into a manageable launch roster and define each faction's playable difference.
14. World persistence: define resource respawn/depletion, faction territory changes, off-screen simulation, and safehouse vulnerability.
15. Companions: choose launch species and define injury, death, recovery, replacement, breeding, and commands.
16. Content: define mature-content boundaries, age-rating targets, consent rules, and platform constraints before producing adult material. Baseline here remains non-explicit.
17. Monetization and commercial assumptions: validate audience, costs, browser payment/distribution options, and storefront rules; concept claims of uniqueness or market gap are unverified hypotheses, not evidence.
18. Remove or separately file unrelated PotGrowHub SEO/AR/VR product concepts, website briefs, repeated prototype code, promotional copy, and unverified claims about competitors.

# 🌋 OVERVIEW


**Phoenix Blaze** is an innovative HTML5-based open-world sandbox survival game where you play as a newly-turned zombie who retains human consciousness.


Manage your bloodlust with cannabis-based crafting, blend in with both humans and zombies, and navigate a post-apocalyptic world of survival, moral choices, and self-discovery.


You aren't just another zombie.


You're something different.


You're caught between two worlds: the instinct to consume and the desire to remain human.


Every decision matters. Every encounter carries consequences. Every survivor has a story.


<details>
<summary>🔥 <strong>THE PHOENIX BLAZE PREMISE</strong></summary>


A new existence begins after death.


Your character awakens as a zombie, but something unexpected has happened: your consciousness survived the transformation.


You retain memories, emotions, reasoning, and the ability to make choices.


However, your body has developed instincts that constantly threaten your remaining humanity.


The challenge isn't simply surviving the apocalypse.


It's deciding what kind of creature you will become.


</details>


## 🎯 Core Features


| Feature | Description | Category |
|---|---|---|
| 🧟 **Play as a Conscious Zombie** | Retain fragments of humanity while battling zombie instincts | Character |
| 🍃 **Cannabis Integration** | Craft marijuana-based items to suppress bloodlust and enhance abilities | Crafting |
| 🎭 **Stealth & Disguise** | Blend in with humans or mimic zombie behavior | Stealth |
| 🔨 **Deep Crafting System** | Create tools, weapons, shelters, and survival gear from gathered materials | Survival |
| 🌍 **Open-World Exploration** | Semi-open world with 6-8 diverse regions | Exploration |
| 🤖 **AI-Driven NPCs** | Dynamic interactions with human survivors and other zombies | Intelligence |
| 🎮 **Moral Decision-Making** | Branching storylines based on player choices | Story |
| 🔞 **Mature Content** | NSFW character customization and relationship mechanics | Mature |


---


# 🧠 GAME IDENTITY


<details>
<summary>🧟 <strong>THE PLAYER: HUMANITY INSIDE THE UNDEAD</strong></summary>


The player character occupies a unique position in the post-apocalyptic world.


**Human characteristics:**
- Memories and identity.
- Reasoning and decision-making.
- Emotional responses.
- Social interaction.
- Moral judgment.


**Zombie characteristics:**
- Bloodlust and hunger.
- Physical deterioration.
- Instinctive behavior.
- Hostile zombie encounters.
- Survival-driven abilities.


The tension between these characteristics creates the central gameplay experience.


</details>


<details>
<summary>🍃 <strong>CANNABIS-BASED SURVIVAL CRAFTING</strong></summary>


Cannabis integration is a distinctive gameplay mechanic.


It provides a crafting and progression system involving fictional consumables, recipes, and survival applications.


Potential mechanics include:


- Bloodlust management.
- Temporary ability enhancements.
- Craftable calming items.
- Resource gathering.
- Recipe discovery.
- Character progression.


</details>


<details>
<summary>🎭 <strong>STEALTH, DISGUISE & SOCIAL SURVIVAL</strong></summary>


The player must navigate encounters with two fundamentally different populations.


**Human survivors**
- Suspicion and trust.
- Social interactions.
- Disguise recognition.
- Reputation and consequences.


**Zombie populations**
- Behavioral mimicry.
- Threat detection.
- Hostile encounters.
- Environmental awareness.


</details>


---


# 🏗️ PROJECT STRUCTURE


The project follows a modular architecture designed to separate gameplay logic, entities, assets, interface components, and supporting utilities.


```text
zombie/
│
├── src/
│   │
│   ├── game/
│   │   ├── core/           # Core game engine
│   │   ├── systems/        # Game systems (crafting, combat, AI)
│   │   ├── entities/       # Player, enemies, NPCs
│   │   └── ui/             # HUD, menus, inventory
│   │
│   ├── assets/             # Sprites, audio, configs
│   ├── utils/              # Helper functions
│   └── index.html          # Main entry point
│
├── docs/                   # Documentation
├── tests/                  # Unit and integration tests
├── config/                 # Configuration files
├── package.json
└── README.md
```


## 📂 Directory Responsibilities


| Directory | Responsibility |
|---|---|
| `src/game/core/` | Core game engine and initialization |
| `src/game/systems/` | Crafting, combat, AI, and gameplay logic |
| `src/game/entities/` | Player, enemies, and NPC entities |
| `src/game/ui/` | HUD, menus, and inventory |
| `src/assets/` | Sprites, audio, and configurations |
| `src/utils/` | Shared helper functions |
| `docs/` | Technical and gameplay documentation |
| `tests/` | Automated testing |
| `config/` | Project configuration |


---


# ⚙️ TECHNOLOGY STACK


The technology foundation uses established web-development tools and a browser-based 2D game engine.


| Component | Technology | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| 🎮 Framework | Phaser 3 | 2D game engine for web |
| 💻 Language | JavaScript (ES6+) | Game logic and application code |
| 📦 Build Tool | Webpack / Parcel | Bundling and development builds |
| 🧪 Testing | Jest | Automated testing |
| 🌿 Version Control | Git | Source control and collaboration |
| 🌐 Platform | HTML5 | Browser-based delivery |


### 🧩 Technology Highlights


- **Phaser 3:** Scene management, rendering, physics, input, and game objects.
- **JavaScript ES6+:** Modern modular application development.
- **Webpack/Parcel:** Asset processing and production bundling.
- **Jest:** Automated unit and integration testing.
- **Git:** Version history and collaborative development.

# 🚀 GETTING STARTED


Ready to rise from the grave?


Follow the instructions below to get the project running locally.


## 📋 Prerequisites


| Requirement | Version |
|---|---|
| Node.js | v14+ |
| npm | Compatible with installed Node.js |
| Yarn | Optional alternative package manager |
| Git | Recommended |


## 📥 Installation


### Step 1: Clone the repository


```bash
git clone https://github.com/reeferguyai/zombie.git
```


### Step 2: Enter the project directory


```bash
cd zombie
```


### Step 3: Install dependencies


```bash
npm install
```


### Step 4: Start the development server


```bash
npm run dev
```


### Step 5: Build for production


```bash
npm run build
```


## 🖥️ Development Commands


| Command | Action |
|---|---|
| `npm install` | Install project dependencies |
| `npm run dev` | Start development server |
| `npm run build` | Create production build |


> 💡 **Development Note:** The actual available scripts are determined by `package.json`. The commands above preserve the original project's documented setup.

# 🧪 TESTING & QUALITY


Testing is essential for maintaining stability as the game grows.


The original project identifies Jest as its testing framework.


### Testing Categories


| Test Category | Purpose |
|---|---|
| Unit Testing | Verify individual functions and components |
| Integration Testing | Verify interactions between gameplay systems |
| Gameplay Testing | Validate player-facing mechanics |
| UI Testing | Verify menus, HUD, and inventory behavior |
| Performance Testing | Identify rendering and processing bottlenecks |
| Regression Testing | Ensure updates don't break existing functionality |


### Quality Goals


- Consistent gameplay behavior.
- Stable game initialization.
- Reliable inventory operations.
- Predictable NPC interactions.
- Smooth browser performance.
- Functional production builds.

# 📖 GLOSSARY


A quick reference to terminology used throughout Phoenix Blaze.


| Term | Definition |
|---|---|
| 🧟 Conscious Zombie | A zombie character who retains human awareness |
| 🩸 Bloodlust | The instinctive hunger that challenges player control |
| 🍃 Cannabis Integration | Fictional cannabis-based crafting and gameplay mechanics |
| 🎭 Disguise | Techniques used to conceal the player's zombie identity |
| 🤖 NPC | Non-player character controlled by game logic or AI |
| 🌍 Open World | An explorable game environment |
| 🔨 Crafting | Combining gathered resources to create items |
| 🧠 Human Consciousness | The player's retained memories, reasoning, and identity |
| 🎮 Branching Storyline | Narrative progression influenced by player choices |
| 🏕️ Safe Zone | A potential location offering protection from hostile threats |
| 🧪 Phaser 3 | JavaScript framework used for 2D game development |
| 🏗️ Game Engine | The underlying system responsible for running the game |

# 🎬 CREATIVE DIRECTION & WORLDBUILDING


> [!IMPORTANT]
> This section expands the creative brief. It describes design direction and potential systems; it does not claim that unlisted content or mechanics are already implemented.


## 🌋 The Central Creative Pillars


Phoenix Blaze is built around a contradiction: a character who is physically undead but still capable of human thought, feeling, and choice.


| Pillar | Player-facing meaning | Design opportunity |
|---|---|---|
| 🧠 Retained Humanity | The player can remember, reason, care, and choose | Dialogue, relationships, moral dilemmas |
| 🩸 Unwanted Instinct | Zombie urges can challenge deliberate choices | Bloodlust pressure and risk/reward decisions |
| 🎭 Dual Identity | The player may need to pass among humans and zombies | Suspicion, disguise, behavioral systems |
| 🌍 Uncertain World | Familiar places have become dangerous and changed | Exploration, environmental storytelling |
| 🍃 Unusual Survival Tools | Cannabis-inspired fictional crafting has a distinct role | Recipes, resources, temporary effects |
| 🔥 Consequences | Choices can alter relationships and story outcomes | Branching narrative and persistent flags |


## 🎭 Tone, Mood & Narrative Voice


The world should feel dangerous, strange, darkly humorous, and occasionally hopeful. Humor can emerge from the absurdity of trying to maintain ordinary habits after becoming undead, while serious moments should give the player's decisions emotional weight.


**Suggested tone spectrum:**


- **Horror:** uncertainty, isolation, danger, and the unknown.
- **Survival:** scarcity, preparation, improvisation, and risk.
- **Dark comedy:** strange situations, awkward disguises, and undead inconveniences.
- **Drama:** trust, loss, identity, guilt, and redemption.
- **Exploration:** curiosity, discovery, secrets, and environmental clues.
- **Hope:** rebuilding, cooperation, and the possibility of change.


## 📚 Narrative Themes


1. **Identity:** What makes someone human: biology, memory, behavior, or choice?
2. **Control:** Can instinct be managed, redirected, or accepted?
3. **Belonging:** Where does a conscious zombie fit in a divided world?
4. **Trust:** How much truth can the player safely reveal?
5. **Responsibility:** What obligations remain after the old world collapses?
6. **Transformation:** Is becoming something different necessarily becoming something worse?


## 🎞️ Environmental Storytelling


The environment can communicate history without requiring lengthy exposition.


| Environmental clue | Possible story information |
|---|---|
| Barricaded storefront | A hurried attempt to contain a threat |
| Abandoned radio station | A former communication hub or hidden broadcast |
| Personal notes | A survivor's fears, plans, or final message |
| Improvised shelter | Evidence of recent human activity |
| Overgrown streets | Time, neglect, and the return of nature |
| Damaged warning signs | A past emergency or failed containment effort |
| Empty supply cache | Someone arrived before the player |


---


# 🗺️ WORLD REGIONS & ENVIRONMENT DESIGN


The original concept calls for a semi-open world with **6-8 diverse regions**. The following is a planning framework for developing those regions. Names and details below are proposals, not confirmed existing game locations.


## 🌎 Region Design Matrix


| Region concept | Atmosphere | Exploration opportunities | Potential hazards |
|---|---|---|---|
| 🏙️ Abandoned Downtown | Dense, urban, overgrown | Shops, rooftops, records, supplies | Narrow routes, hostile groups |
| 🏡 Suburban Ruins | Quiet, familiar, unsettling | Homes, garages, personal stories | Ambushes, unstable structures |
| 🌲 Deep Greenbelt | Wild, overgrown, isolated | Natural resources, hidden paths | Poor visibility, difficult navigation |
| 🏭 Industrial Quarter | Rusted, loud, hazardous | Tools, machinery, workshops | Environmental hazards, confined spaces |
| 🏥 Medical District | Clinical, tense, abandoned | Records, medical supplies, clues | Locked areas, dangerous encounters |
| 🚧 Highway Expanse | Open, exposed, weathered | Vehicles, roadside caches, routes | Limited cover, blocked roads |
| 🏕️ Survivor Enclave | Organized, guarded, suspicious | NPCs, missions, trading concepts | Social tension, faction rules |
| ⚓ Waterfront / Flood Zone | Damp, foggy, unstable | Docks, warehouses, boats, secrets | Water hazards, restricted access |


## 🧭 Region Specification Template


Use this template when designing each region:


```yaml
region:
  id: "region_unique_id"
  name: "Region Name"
  theme: "Atmosphere and visual identity"
  description: "Short player-facing description"
  points_of_interest:
    - id: "poi_01"
      name: "Point of Interest"
      type: "landmark"
  resources: []
  hazards: []
  npc_groups: []
  zombie_groups: []
  missions: []
  entry_conditions: []
  exit_routes: []
  ambient_audio: []
  lighting_profile: "profile_name"
  discovered: false
```


## 🧱 Environment Design Checklist


- [ ] Establish a distinct visual identity for the region.
- [ ] Define its entry points and exits.
- [ ] Identify landmarks and navigation cues.
- [ ] Place resource opportunities.
- [ ] Define environmental hazards.
- [ ] Add optional discoveries and narrative clues.
- [ ] Establish ambient audio and lighting.
- [ ] Identify relevant NPC and zombie populations.
- [ ] Test navigation and encounter readability.
- [ ] Confirm that the region supports the intended story and gameplay.

# 🧍 CHARACTER DESIGN & PROGRESSION


## 🧠 Character Identity Model


The player character is the central bridge between the human and zombie sides of the game.


Potential character data categories:


| Category | Example data | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| Identity | Name, appearance, background | Player expression |
| Condition | Health, stamina, status effects | Survival state |
| Instinct | Bloodlust and hunger state | Zombie pressure |
| Humanity | Narrative traits and retained memories | Roleplaying and story |
| Social | Trust, reputation, relationship flags | NPC interactions |
| Progression | Experience, unlocked abilities | Long-term development |
| Equipment | Equipped items and carried gear | Gameplay capability |


## 📈 Progression Philosophy


Progression should create meaningful options rather than simply increasing every statistic.


Possible progression paths:


- **The Survivor:** resourcefulness, preparation, and endurance.
- **The Ghost:** stealth, disguise, and avoiding confrontation.
- **The Mediator:** communication, trust, and relationship building.
- **The Instinctive:** understanding and managing zombie abilities.
- **The Tinkerer:** crafting knowledge, equipment, and improvised tools.


These are optional design archetypes. They are not confirmed classes or implemented skill trees.


## 🎨 Character Customization Categories


| Category | Possible options |
|---|---|
| Appearance | Hair, facial details, clothing, wear and damage |
| Undead presentation | Skin condition, scars, movement style |
| Clothing | Everyday, improvised, protective, faction-inspired |
| Personality | Dialogue tone and roleplaying choices |
| Background | Pre-apocalypse occupation or personal history |
| Relationships | Social history and narrative connections |


---


# 🧟 ZOMBIE ECOLOGY & FACTIONS


A varied undead population can make the world feel more alive and unpredictable.


## 🧬 Proposed Zombie Behavior Archetypes


| Archetype | Behavior concept | Gameplay implication |
|---|---|---|
| 🧟 Drifter | Wanders without a clear destination | Environmental presence |
| 👂 Listener | Reacts strongly to sound | Noise-aware stealth |
| 🏃 Pursuer | Moves toward detected targets | Chase encounters |
| 🛡️ Brute | Slow, durable, physically imposing | High-risk confrontation |
| 👥 Swarm | Groups around activity or stimuli | Crowd management |
| 🧠 Remnant | Displays fragments of recognizable behavior | Narrative discovery |


These archetypes are creative proposals, not a confirmed enemy roster.


## 🔊 Perception & Awareness Concepts


Zombie behavior could respond to:


- Sound intensity and distance.
- Line of sight.
- Movement and proximity.
- Environmental obstructions.
- Recent disturbances.
- Group behavior and nearby threats.


## 🧭 Faction Planning Template


```yaml
faction:
  id: "faction_id"
  name: "Faction Name"
  description: "Faction background"
  alignment: "unknown"
  territory: []
  leader: null
  allies: []
  rivals: []
  player_reputation: 0
  known_to_player: false
```


---


# 🧑‍🤝‍🧑 HUMAN SURVIVORS & RELATIONSHIPS


Human survivors should be more than obstacles or quest dispensers. Their behavior, needs, fears, and histories can create meaningful encounters.


## 🤝 Relationship Dimensions


| Dimension | Meaning | Possible result |
|---|---|---|
| Trust | Confidence in the player | Access to information or shelter |
| Suspicion | Concern about the player's identity | Questions, restrictions, confrontation |
| Reputation | Community-wide perception | Different treatment across a group |
| Affinity | Personal relationship strength | Optional dialogue and story branches |
| Fear | Perceived danger | Avoidance or defensive behavior |
| Respect | Recognition of actions and competence | Cooperation or leadership opportunities |


## 💬 Dialogue Design


A dialogue interaction may contain:


1. Opening line.
2. Context-aware response options.
3. Information exchange.
4. A choice or action.
5. A reaction.
6. A relationship or story-state update.


## 🧩 Example Dialogue State


```json
{
  "npc_id": "survivor_001",
  "relationship": {
    "trust": 10,
    "suspicion": 35,
    "affinity": 0,
    "fear": 15
  },
  "flags": {
    "met_player": true,
    "knows_secret": false,
    "quest_completed": false
  }
}
```


This is an illustrative data structure, not an existing save format.

# 🎒 INVENTORY, LOOT & EQUIPMENT


## 📦 Item Categories


| Category | Examples | Possible use |
|---|---|---|
| Resources | Scrap, cloth, wood | Crafting |
| Tools | Improvised tools | Gathering and interaction |
| Weapons | Melee or ranged equipment | Combat |
| Clothing | Jackets, boots, protective wear | Character appearance and protection |
| Consumables | Food, water, fictional crafted items | Survival and status management |
| Quest Items | Notes, keys, evidence | Narrative progression |
| Collectibles | Memorabilia, records, unusual objects | Discovery and optional rewards |


## 🧰 Inventory Design Considerations


- Item stacking rules.
- Carrying capacity.
- Item weight or size, if used.
- Equipment slots.
- Quick-access slots.
- Item descriptions and inspection.
- Sorting and filtering.
- Transfer between containers.
- Item durability, if used.
- Clear feedback for unavailable actions.


## 🏷️ Suggested Item Metadata


```json
{
  "id": "item_unique_id",
  "name": "Example Item",
  "category": "resource",
  "description": "A sample item description.",
  "stackable": true,
  "quantity": 1,
  "rarity": "common",
  "icon": "assets/icons/example.png",
  "tags": ["resource", "crafting"]
}
```

# 🛠️ CRAFTING, RECIPES & RESOURCES


Crafting is one of the distinctive survival systems in Phoenix Blaze.


## 🔨 Crafting Loop


```mermaid
flowchart TD
    A["Explore the world"] --> B["Find resources"]
    B --> C["Collect materials"]
    C --> D["Discover or select a recipe"]
    D --> E{"Requirements met?"}
    E -->|Yes| F["Craft the item"]
    E -->|No| G["Continue gathering"]
    G --> B
    F --> H["Use, store, or trade item"]
    H --> A
```


## 🌿 Recipe Categories


- Survival equipment.
- Tools and repair materials.
- Shelter components.
- Improvised gear.
- Fictional cannabis-inspired items.
- Character utility items.
- Decorative and collectible objects.


## 🧪 Recipe Data Template


```json
{
  "id": "recipe_unique_id",
  "name": "Example Recipe",
  "category": "survival",
  "description": "An example crafting recipe.",
  "ingredients": [
    {
      "item_id": "material_001",
      "quantity": 2
    }
  ],
  "output": {
    "item_id": "crafted_item_001",
    "quantity": 1
  },
  "station_required": null,
  "unlock_condition": null
}
```


## ♻️ Resource Economy Principles


- Resources should have understandable uses.
- Common materials should support routine crafting.
- Rare materials should justify exploration.
- Recipes should communicate requirements clearly.
- Item descriptions should explain practical value.
- Crafting should offer meaningful choices rather than unnecessary repetition.

# 🧭 EXPLORATION, MISSIONS & EVENTS


## 🎯 Mission Categories


| Mission type | Description |
|---|---|
| Main Story | Advances the central narrative |
| Side Mission | Adds optional character or world stories |
| Discovery | Rewards exploration and investigation |
| Collection | Encourages finding specific objects |
| Survival | Focuses on obtaining or protecting resources |
| Relationship | Develops NPC connections |
| Dynamic Event | Introduces a temporary world situation |


## 🗺️ Mission Lifecycle


```mermaid
flowchart TD
    A["Mission becomes available"] --> B["Player discovers mission"]
    B --> C["Review objective"]
    C --> D["Travel to location"]
    D --> E["Complete objective"]
    E --> F["Resolve consequences"]
    F --> G["Award outcome or reward"]
    G --> H["Update world and story state"]
```


## 📌 Mission Specification Template


```yaml
mission:
  id: "mission_unique_id"
  title: "Mission Title"
  description: "Player-facing mission summary"
  category: "side"
  objectives: []
  prerequisites: []
  locations: []
  rewards: []
  consequences: []
  repeatable: false
  status: "locked"
```

# 🧩 USER INTERFACE & ACCESSIBILITY


## 🖥️ Interface Areas


| Interface | Information or actions |
|---|---|
| Main Menu | Start, continue, settings, credits |
| HUD | Player condition and immediate gameplay information |
| Inventory | Items, equipment, and storage |
| Crafting Menu | Recipes, materials, and crafting actions |
| Map | Regions, landmarks, and discovered locations |
| Journal | Missions, notes, and discoveries |
| Dialogue | NPC conversation and choices |
| Settings | Audio, controls, display, and accessibility |
| Pause Menu | Resume, settings, save options where supported |


## 🎨 Visual Hierarchy


Recommended interface principles:


- Use consistent heading levels.
- Reserve strong colors for important states.
- Distinguish interactive elements from decorative elements.
- Keep status indicators understandable.
- Use icons alongside text, not as the only source of meaning.
- Maintain readable contrast.
- Keep frequently used actions easy to reach.
- Avoid covering important gameplay information.


## ♿ Accessibility Checklist


- [ ] Keyboard navigation where applicable.
- [ ] Remappable controls where feasible.
- [ ] Adjustable text size.
- [ ] Readable color contrast.
- [ ] Color-independent status indicators.
- [ ] Captions or subtitles for dialogue and important sounds.
- [ ] Audio volume controls.
- [ ] Reduced-motion option for nonessential effects.
- [ ] Clear focus indicators.
- [ ] Avoid reliance on rapid input alone.
- [ ] Explain controls before requiring them.


---


# 🎧 AUDIO & VISUAL EFFECTS


## 🔊 Audio Layers


| Audio layer | Potential content |
|---|---|
| Ambient | Wind, distant movement, environmental atmosphere |
| Music | Exploration, tension, combat, narrative moments |
| Player | Movement, interaction, status feedback |
| NPC | Dialogue, reactions, calls, background conversation |
| Zombie | Groans, movement, alerts, group sounds |
| Interface | Menu navigation and action confirmation |
| Environmental | Rain, machinery, fire, alarms, weather |


## ✨ Visual Feedback


Potential visual effects include:


- Ember particles and firelight.
- Dust, fog, and atmospheric haze.
- Screen feedback for important status changes.
- Animated HUD transitions.
- Environmental lighting changes.
- Impact effects and movement trails.
- Distinct interaction highlights.
- Clear visual cues for threats and discoveries.


## 🎞️ Animation Principles


1. Prioritize readable movement.
2. Keep gameplay-critical animations responsive.
3. Use transitions to communicate state changes.
4. Avoid excessive screen effects.
5. Provide reduced-motion settings where practical.
6. Keep animation performance within browser limits.


---


# 💾 SAVE SYSTEM & GAME DATA


A persistence system can preserve player progress between sessions.


## 💽 Potential Save Categories


| Data category | Example |
|---|---|
| Player | Position, condition, progression |
| Inventory | Items, quantities, equipment |
| World | Discovered regions and changed objects |
| Missions | Active, completed, and failed objectives |
| Relationships | Trust and story flags |
| Settings | Audio, controls, display preferences |
| Narrative | Choices and branching story state |


## 🧾 Save Design Requirements


- Clearly communicate when a save succeeds.
- Handle unavailable or invalid save data.
- Avoid overwriting a valid save without confirmation.
- Consider multiple save slots.
- Consider versioning save data.
- Validate loaded data before using it.
- Provide a recovery strategy for corrupted data.


## 🧩 Example Save Structure


```json
{
  "save_version": 1,
  "saved_at": "ISO-8601 timestamp",
  "player": {},
  "inventory": [],
  "world_state": {},
  "missions": {},
  "relationships": {},
  "settings": {}
}
```


The structure is illustrative and does not prescribe a currently implemented save format.


---


# ⚖️ BALANCE, DIFFICULTY & PLAYER CHOICE


## 🎚️ Difficulty Considerations


| Area | Design question |
|---|---|
| Survival pressure | How often must the player manage resources? |
| Bloodlust | How demanding should instinct management become? |
| Combat | How costly should direct confrontation be? |
| Stealth | How forgiving should detection systems be? |
| Crafting | How much preparation should recipes require? |
| Exploration | How much guidance should the map provide? |
| Narrative | How visible should choice consequences be? |


## 🧭 Choice & Consequence Principles


- Choices should have understandable context.
- Consequences may be immediate or delayed.
- Not every decision needs a clearly good or bad answer.
- NPC reactions should be consistent with known events.
- Major story decisions should be recorded in game state.
- The player should be able to understand why an outcome occurred.


---


# 🔒 SECURITY, PRIVACY & MATURE-CONTENT BOUNDARIES


Phoenix Blaze identifies mature character customization and relationship mechanics as part of its concept.


Any implementation should distinguish fictional mature themes from real-world user safety and platform requirements.


## 🔞 Mature-Content Planning


- Clearly communicate mature themes before gameplay.
- Consider optional content settings.
- Avoid unexpected explicit content.
- Keep content labels accurate.
- Establish age-appropriate access controls for distribution.
- Review platform rules before release.


## 🛡️ Technical Safety


- Validate imported or user-provided data.
- Avoid placing secrets in browser-delivered source code.
- Treat local save data as untrusted input.
- Handle errors without exposing sensitive information.
- Keep dependencies updated.
- Review third-party assets and licenses.
- Do not assume client-side checks provide server-grade security.


---


# 🚦 PERFORMANCE & BROWSER COMPATIBILITY


## ⚡ Performance Areas


| Area | Optimization opportunity |
|---|---|
| Rendering | Limit unnecessary redraws and expensive effects |
| Assets | Compress and appropriately size images and audio |
| Memory | Release unused objects and listeners |
| Physics | Avoid unnecessary collision calculations |
| AI | Update distant entities less frequently where appropriate |
| Loading | Load assets by scene or region when practical |
| UI | Avoid unnecessary interface reconstruction |
| Audio | Manage playback and audio resources carefully |


## 🌐 Browser Validation Checklist


- [ ] Verify game startup in supported browsers.
- [ ] Verify canvas rendering.
- [ ] Verify keyboard and pointer input.
- [ ] Verify audio behavior and browser restrictions.
- [ ] Verify asset loading paths.
- [ ] Verify production build output.
- [ ] Verify resizing and different display dimensions.
- [ ] Verify save and restore behavior if implemented.
- [ ] Verify performance during extended sessions.


Actual browser support should be established through testing rather than assumed.

# 🧱 ARCHITECTURE & DATA FLOW


The original project structure separates core engine code, systems, entities, UI, assets, utilities, documentation, tests, and configuration. The following diagram expands that modular concept.


```mermaid
flowchart TB
    A["HTML5 Entry Point"] --> B["Game Core"]
    B --> C["Scene Management"]
    B --> D["Input Management"]
    B --> E["Asset Management"]
    C --> F["Gameplay Systems"]
    F --> G["Character & Entity Systems"]
    F --> H["Crafting & Inventory"]
    F --> I["Combat & Survival"]
    F --> J["NPC & AI"]
    F --> K["World & Missions"]
    G --> L["User Interface"]
    H --> L
    I --> L
    J --> L
    K --> L
    F --> M["Save / Persistence Layer"]
    E --> N["Sprites, Audio & Config"]
```


## 🧩 Suggested Module Responsibilities


| Module | Responsibility | Example boundary |
|---|---|---|
| Core | Startup and lifecycle | Initialize and shut down the game |
| Scenes | Scene transitions | Menu, world, pause |
| Input | Input mapping | Keyboard and pointer actions |
| Entities | Player, NPCs, enemies | Entity state and behavior |
| Systems | Rules and mechanics | Crafting, combat, stealth |
| UI | Player-facing presentation | HUD, menus, inventory |
| Assets | Resource loading | Images, audio, configuration |
| Utilities | Shared helpers | Reusable functions |
| Persistence | Save and restore | Serialization and validation |


## 🔄 Architectural Guidelines


- Keep systems focused on one responsibility.
- Avoid tightly coupling UI elements to game rules.
- Use clear interfaces between systems.
- Keep configuration separate from runtime state.
- Make important game state changes testable.
- Document dependencies between major systems.

# 🧰 DEVELOPER WORKFLOW & GIT PRACTICES


## 🌿 Suggested Branching Model


| Branch type | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `main` | Stable project line |
| `develop` | Integration work, if adopted |
| `feature/*` | New functionality |
| `fix/*` | Bug fixes |
| `docs/*` | Documentation changes |
| `release/*` | Release preparation |


This is a suggested workflow, not a claim about the repository's current branch configuration.


## 📝 Commit Message Examples


```text
feat: add player movement
fix: correct inventory item stacking
docs: expand crafting documentation
test: add bloodlust system tests
refactor: separate NPC behavior logic
style: improve HUD presentation
```


## 🔍 Pull Request Checklist


- [ ] Describe the change.
- [ ] Explain why it is needed.
- [ ] Identify affected systems.
- [ ] Include test results.
- [ ] Include screenshots or recordings for visual changes where useful.
- [ ] Update documentation.
- [ ] Check for unrelated file changes.
- [ ] Confirm that existing functionality remains intact.


---


# 📦 RELEASE & BUILD CHECKLIST


## 🏗️ Before Building


- [ ] Confirm dependencies are installed.
- [ ] Review configuration.
- [ ] Check asset references.
- [ ] Check for accidental debug-only behavior.
- [ ] Run available tests.
- [ ] Verify the development build.


## 🚀 Before Release


- [ ] Run `npm run build`.
- [ ] Verify generated output.
- [ ] Test the production build in a browser.
- [ ] Verify menus and core interactions.
- [ ] Verify audio and asset loading.
- [ ] Review mature-content disclosures.
- [ ] Review third-party asset licenses.
- [ ] Document known issues.
- [ ] Prepare release notes.
- [ ] Preserve a backup of the release artifact.


## 🏷️ Release Notes Template


```markdown
# Phoenix Blaze Release Notes


## Version
[Version number]


## Release Date
[Date]


## Added
- [New functionality]


## Changed
- [Updated functionality]


## Fixed
- [Bug fixes]


## Known Issues
- [Known limitations]


## Compatibility
- [Tested browsers and environments]
```

# 🧾 REQUIREMENTS & ACCEPTANCE CRITERIA


A feature should have a clear purpose and a testable completion condition.


## 📋 Feature Definition Template


```markdown
## Feature: [Feature Name]


### Purpose
What problem does this feature solve?


### Player Experience
What does the player see, hear, or do?


### Functional Requirements
- Requirement 1
- Requirement 2
- Requirement 3


### Dependencies
- Related systems
- Required assets
- Required data


### Acceptance Criteria
- [ ] Expected behavior occurs.
- [ ] Invalid actions are handled.
- [ ] Feedback is understandable.
- [ ] Relevant tests pass.
- [ ] Documentation is updated.


### Status
Proposed / Planned / In Progress / Complete
```


## 🚦 Documentation Status Labels


| Label | Meaning |
|---|---|
| <span style="color:green">🟢 Complete</span> | Implemented and verified |
| 🟡 In Progress | Currently being developed |
| 🔵 Planned | Accepted for future work |
| ⚪ Proposed | Under consideration |
| 🔴 Blocked | Cannot proceed until an issue is resolved |
| 🟣 Needs Review | Requires testing or approval |

# 🧪 TEST SCENARIOS


The following scenarios provide examples of behavior that can be validated as systems are implemented.


| ID | Scenario | Expected result |
|---|---|---|
| PB-001 | Start a new game | The game enters the intended starting scene |
| PB-002 | Move the player | Player movement responds to configured input |
| PB-003 | Open inventory | Inventory displays available items |
| PB-004 | Attempt an unavailable recipe | The game explains which requirements are missing |
| PB-005 | Complete a crafting recipe | Required materials are consumed and output is created |
| PB-006 | Trigger a zombie encounter | The appropriate encounter behavior occurs |
| PB-007 | Enter an NPC conversation | Dialogue and response options appear |
| PB-008 | Make a story choice | The selected choice is recorded |
| PB-009 | Discover a region | Discovery state updates |
| PB-010 | Save progress | Save feedback is shown and data is stored |
| PB-011 | Restore progress | Valid saved state is restored |
| PB-012 | Load invalid save data | The game handles the error safely |


## 🐞 Bug Report Template


```markdown
## Bug Title
[Short description]


## Environment
- Operating system:
- Browser:
- Game version:


## Steps to Reproduce
1.
2.
3.


## Expected Behavior
[What should happen]


## Actual Behavior
[What happened]


## Evidence
[Screenshots, logs, or recordings]


## Severity
Low / Medium / High / Critical
```

# 🗓️ EXPANDED MILESTONES


This section expands the original four-phase, 16-week roadmap into more detailed planning categories. It does not change the original roadmap or mark additional tasks as completed.


| Milestone | Focus | Example deliverables |
|---|---|---|
| M1 | Project foundation | Startup, scene structure, configuration |
| M2 | Player foundation | Character creation and movement |
| M3 | Survival foundation | Inventory and basic resource handling |
| M4 | Core pressure systems | Bloodlust and survival logic |
| M5 | Interaction | NPC and dialogue foundations |
| M6 | World foundation | Region layout and exploration |
| M7 | Content expansion | Recipes, missions, and narrative |
| M8 | Presentation | HUD, audio, effects, and menus |
| M9 | Quality | Testing, balancing, and optimization |
| M10 | Beta preparation | Release build and feedback process |


## 📍 Milestone Review Questions


At the end of each milestone, ask:


1. What was intended?
2. What was actually completed?
3. What has been tested?
4. What remains incomplete?
5. What blockers were discovered?
6. What should be prioritized next?
7. Does the documentation reflect the current implementation?

# ❓ FAQ


<details>
<summary><strong>🧟 What is Phoenix Blaze?</strong></summary>


Phoenix Blaze is an HTML5-based open-world sandbox survival game centered on a newly turned zombie who retains human consciousness.


</details>


<details>
<summary><strong>🎮 What kind of game is it?</strong></summary>


It combines survival, exploration, crafting, stealth, AI-driven NPC interactions, and branching moral decisions.


</details>


<details>
<summary><strong>🍃 What role does cannabis play?</strong></summary>


Cannabis-based fictional crafting is a distinctive game mechanic associated with bloodlust management and potential ability effects.


</details>


<details>
<summary><strong>🌍 How large is the world?</strong></summary>


The original concept specifies a semi-open world with 6-8 diverse regions.


</details>


<details>
<summary><strong>🤖 Will NPCs use AI?</strong></summary>


AI-driven NPC interactions with human survivors and other zombies are part of the core feature concept. The exact implementation remains a development decision.


</details>


<details>
<summary><strong>🛠️ What engine does the project use?</strong></summary>


The documented technology stack identifies Phaser 3 as the 2D web game framework.


</details>


<details>
<summary><strong>💻 How do I run the project?</strong></summary>


The original setup instructions use Node.js, npm, `npm install`, and `npm run dev`.


</details>


<details>
<summary><strong>🔐 Is the project open source?</strong></summary>


The supplied license statement is Private - All rights reserved.


</details>
