How the backstory and outbreak are revealed: Uncover it gradually through exploration rather than one long explanation. ChroniX locations, photographs, emails, research terminals, recordings, familiar voices, and former coworkers trigger memory fragments. Each clue adds context about the Euphoria project, the outbreak, and why Phoenix retained her consciousness.


The protagonist’s emotional journey: Phoenix moves from shock and basic survival, to investigating what happened and learning to use her zombie abilities, then toward deciding what her changed identity means. Her arc should leave room for cure-seeking, accepting her undead nature, or becoming a bridge between humans and zombies.


How the human–zombie conflict drives decisions: Make the conflict playable, not just something Phoenix talks about. Bloodlust can threaten her control and relationships; feeding, suppressing the urge, and choosing blood mixtures shape her abilities and identity. The notes also propose competing inner voices—the Scientist, Survivor, Hungry One, and Witness—to express how her instincts and memories pull her in different directions.


How side characters affect the main narrative: Give key characters different, personal stakes in Phoenix’s choices. Marcus, a former EMT, tests whether she can earn human trust; Dr. Lena Voss connects her to the research and the possibility of a cure; Riven, a conscious undead, challenges her to embrace zombie identity. Their quests, trust, and loyalties can reveal different parts of the story and affect who supports Phoenix later.


The central antagonist’s goal: The notes identify Dr. Elias Thorne and the ChroniX Inner Circle as the main opposing force. Their stated aims include recovering research, controlling the outbreak, developing a cure or weapon, and capturing unusual infected people. That directly conflicts with Phoenix’s need to understand what happened and choose her own future. Thorne’s personal motive isn’t fully settled in the notes, so it should be revealed as a mystery rather than treated as established canon.


How flashbacks and story events unfold: Trigger memories when Phoenix encounters meaningful places, objects, research, voices, or former coworkers. Let each recovered memory add something useful in the present—new dialogue, a quest, journal entry, ability, or clue—so flashbacks move both the character and the investigation forward.


Moral consequences of major decisions: Choices should affect more than a good-or-bad score. Sparing or feeding on survivors can change trust and alliances; losing control can endanger companions; blood choices and mutations can grant power while changing how Phoenix looks and how others respond. The consequences should follow the player into future relationships, faction reactions, and the ending.


How factions offer distinct perspectives: The ChroniX Inner Circle seeks control of the research and outbreak; the Resistance prioritizes protecting survivors and rebuilding; the Scavengers focus on controlling resources and territory; the Cultists see infection as evolution; and the Lone Wolves value independence. Their competing beliefs let players see different ideas of what survival—and a future after the outbreak—could mean.


How choices lead to different outcomes: Track decisions across the story: how Phoenix manages bloodlust, which factions and characters she trusts, what she does with ChroniX research, and whether she pursues a cure or embraces her new nature. Possible outcomes in the notes include restoring humanity, embracing the undead, forming a human–zombie alliance, controlling the outbreak, destroying ChroniX, or surviving independently.


Balancing action and narrative: Let survival play create the tension—stealth, scavenging, and combat—then give players quieter stretches for shelter-building, conversations, and memory discoveries. Place major story revelations at meaningful progression points, but let optional exploration and side-character stories fill in the world at the player’s pace.


and flag where the notes leave exact recipes or rules open.


Balancing action and strategy: Combat should be dangerous and relatively brief; the strategy comes from deciding whether to fight, hide, disguise yourself, or use tools and the environment to get through. Between encounters, players plan around crafting, shelter, supplies, and maintaining bloodlust and disguise.


Fresh combat mechanics: Combat should feel specific to playing a zombie. Phoenix can claw, bite, shove, throw, and use improvised weapons; her Calm, Hungry, and Uncontrolled states change her control and behavior. Blood mixtures and mutations can alter combat abilities, while the versatile Multi-Stick offers a useful alternative to fighting with bare hands.


Interacting with the world: Players scavenge and gather by hand or with tools, then craft equipment and develop shelters. Resource quality can vary by biome, weather, time, and tools; crafting can support experimentation and discovered recipes. The Multi-Stick is an early example: it can be upgraded and reused for defense, fishing, digging, and gathering.


Humanity-versus-zombie morality: The notes don’t specify a single morality meter. A stronger fit is to let actions carry consequences: feeding, losing control, or choosing to protect people can change relationships, faction trust, Phoenix’s identity, and available story outcomes. Blood mixtures and permanent mutations can offer power while changing how Phoenix and others are perceived.


Stealth: Stealth is a core way to navigate, not just a combat bonus. Humans and zombies evaluate Phoenix differently through sight, sound, and scent, so a disguise that fools one group may raise suspicion with the other. Players can sneak, hide, use cover, mask scent, mimic zombie behavior, or create distractions.


Time pressure: Bloodlust rises over time and can increase faster around humans, wounded creatures, combat, or certain nighttime and weather conditions. As it grows, Phoenix becomes more aggressive, loses control, and has more difficulty maintaining disguise; hunger can eventually cause damage. Limited carrying capacity and resource freshness add another planning pressure, so players must choose what to carry and when to take risks.


Leveling and ability progression: Build progression around skill branches such as Stealth, Combat, Herbalism, Blood Alchemy, Animal Handling, Crafting, and Social, alongside a limited number of permanent mutation choices. Exploration, crafting, and quests can unlock skills, recipes, and abilities, giving players several ways to grow rather than relying only on combat experience.


Weapon crafting: Start with scavenged natural materials—branches, stone, flint, wood, fiber, and rope—and gradually add materials such as bone, hides, scrap metal, and nails for more advanced gear. The notes specify one exact early recipe: a sharpened Multi-Stick uses one branch and one stone. Other proposed crafts include a stone knife, axe, hammer, spear, bow, and arrows, but their exact ingredient counts still need to be defined. Recipes should account for crafting time, durability, skill requirements, and whether a workbench is needed.


Random encounters: Use randomized encounters to keep travel and scavenging from becoming predictable: zombie groups, human patrols, wildlife, rival scavengers, and environmental events can create changing risks and opportunities. The encounter system should reflect the region and player progression, while remaining fair and readable.


Dynamic quests and exploration incentives: Give players reasons to explore through faction and survivor quests, memory triggers, safehouses, hidden resources, and recipe discoveries. A visit to a location might reveal Phoenix’s past, provide a new crafting option, or change a faction relationship—so exploration rewards both practical preparation and story progress.


Seamless world connections: Connect districts through walkable streets, trails, waterways, and interiors, streaming nearby world chunks as the player moves. The notes call for interconnected regions generated in manageable chunks; seamless loading is a design target, not a confirmed technical implementation. Major story gates can control access without making ordinary travel feel interrupted.


Environmental storytelling: Make locations tell stories before anyone explains them. An abandoned vehicle, barricaded shop, damaged lab, or personal item can show how people reacted to the outbreak; notes, logs, and belongings can reveal Phoenix’s past and what happened at ChroniX.


Visible impact of the apocalypse: Contrast recently devastated city districts—looted shops, damaged streets, and abandoned vehicles—with quieter places where vegetation and wildlife are returning. Over time, changing crops, animal populations, and local faction pressure can show that the world is still shifting after the initial collapse.


Player impact on the environment: The notes call for persistent world simulation and for player actions to affect local ecosystems and faction behavior. A useful rule would be that clearing a zombie group makes an area temporarily safer, while taking supplies leaves that location picked over for a while; faction patrols or new threats can later change the balance. Exact resource respawn and clearing rules still need to be set.


Distinct biomes: Give each region its own risks and reasons to visit. Urban ruins offer dense scavenging and patrol threats; forests provide plants, wildlife, and routes for concealment; swamps and high-risk sites can offer rare resources at greater danger; farms and cannabis gardens support cultivation but depend on weather and protection. The proposed regions include city, forest, industrial, coastal, swamp, farm, mountain, and lab areas.


Dynamic events: Use both systemic and authored events: zombie groups migrating through a route, survivor patrols or camps appearing, faction raids, scavenger encounters, and weather changes that reshape a trip. Procedural generation is proposed for encounters, patrols, wildlife, environmental events, and quests; authored events can give major story moments more weight.


Weather and visibility: Weather should change tactics, not just scenery. Rain can wash away blood or meat disguise while making scent harder to track and sound easier to hear; fog reduces visual detection; storms hinder movement and can make zombies more aggressive. Weather can vary by region and affect plant growth, resources, stealth, and NPC behavior.


Exploration rewards: Reward curiosity with supplies and tools, but also with new information and options: hidden safehouses, unusual resources, lore, research, and crafting recipes. Dangerous locations should offer better potential finds, while abandoned buildings can have varied interiors to make repeat visits less predictable.


A world that feels alive: Let humans patrol, loot, trade, build, flee, and investigate sounds; let zombies roam in groups, follow scent, react to sound, and become more active at night. Factions should hold territory and change their behavior based on reputation and player actions, while weather and scarcity alter when and where encounters happen.


Adapting to player decisions: Track faction reputation, territory, and local world state. If the player weakens or removes a major faction, its patrols and control should decline, creating an opening for another group—or for threats to move in. If the player leaves an area alone, its existing faction presence, dangers, and resource conditions remain. The notes support reactive faction behavior and territory ownership; the specific power-vacuum rules are a recommended extension.


Close and ranged combat: Make melee the signature because Phoenix is a zombie: claws, bites, shoves, and improvised weapons are always available but put her close to danger. Bows and thrown objects offer quieter, limited ranged options to soften or distract enemies. Guns and crossbows aren’t defined in the current notes; if added, they should be scarce and loud enough to create a trade-off rather than replace stealth and melee.


Difficulty tuning: Scale encounters using player progression and recent performance, as the notes propose. Favor changing enemy types, positions, and group behavior over simply adding more enemies; give attacks readable tells and enough escape options to keep fights challenging but fair.


Zombie abilities: Build encounters around distinct enemy roles: Runners pressure the player’s space, Brutes force dodges or repositioning, and Spitters make standing at range unsafe. Lab Mutants can add less predictable special attacks. The notes name these enemy types but don’t define their attack kits; acid spit could be one option, while explosive blood would be a new mechanic and should have a clear warning before it triggers.


Environmental hazards: Use tight rooms, broken-glass or noisy surfaces, traps set by humans, and objects that can help or hinder movement to make positioning matter. Let players use the environment to break line of sight, create distractions, or escape a crowd. Explosives aren’t a defined combat system yet, so treat them as a later addition rather than a core assumption.


Cannabis in combat: Treat cannabis as a preparation and control tool, not a direct attack. Its documented effects include reducing bloodlust and pausing its increase temporarily; different fictional strains can also affect stamina, perception, decay, or mutations. Use it to help Phoenix stay controlled before or after a fight, with any direct strength or healing boost clearly tied to a specific strain or upgrade.


Weapon crafting and upgrades: Start with the Multi-Stick and sharpened stick, then expand to spears, bows, and other improvised tools as players discover recipes and gather materials. Weapon durability and upgrades should change how players approach encounters; for example, a stronger weapon may hit harder, while a lighter or quieter setup may better suit stealth.


Stealth combat: Let players sneak past enemies or isolate a target using cover, distraction, and disguise. Quiet takedowns would fit the system, but they aren’t fully specified in the notes; if added, they should still carry risk—biting and feeding can increase bloodlust and leave a disguise penalty, potentially drawing attention afterward.


Combat rewards: Enemies can drop useful supplies and materials, while combat milestones can contribute to experience, skills, and mutation progression. Keep rewards varied: fighting may provide resources, but exploration, quests, and crafting should also advance the character so combat isn’t the only profitable playstyle.


Enemy tactics: Zombies should detect and pursue using sound, sight, and scent, with group behavior and greater aggression at night. Human factions can patrol, investigate suspicious activity, set traps, flee, or coordinate in groups. That makes the same fight play differently depending on who notices Phoenix and how.


Combat rhythm: Alternate quick, intense encounters with opportunities to pause, scout, craft, and choose an approach. Phoenix’s Calm, Hungry, and Uncontrolled states provide a natural shift in tempo: hunger can increase aggression and strength, but losing control risks attacking allies and undermining stealth.


Intuitive crafting: Show known recipes in a clear menu that marks available ingredients, missing items, crafting time, skill requirements, and whether a station is needed. Let players make simple known items from their inventory; reserve workbenches or other stations for advanced gear. A quick-access hotbar can keep frequently used tools and supplies within reach.


Key resources: Early recipes use branches, wood, stone, flint, grass, hemp, fiber, vines, and rope. As players progress, they can use bone, feathers, hides, scrap metal, nails, fabric, bottles, and containers. Seeds and cannabis materials support cultivation and specialized recipes.


Upgrading crafted items: Improve equipment through recipe variants and quality tiers, with trade-offs in damage, durability, weight, or stealth. The Multi-Stick can progress from a basic branch to sharpened, fire-hardened, stone-tipped, or reinforced forms; workbench upgrades and skill investment can improve other gear.


Crafting for survival: Make the essentials matter: a campfire, water container and filter, lean-to, storage, tools, weapons, traps, and protective clothing help players survive hostile areas. Workbenches and shelter upgrades support more advanced crafting and recovery, while crafted cannabis products can help manage bloodlust or decay.


Cannabis recipes and balance: Build recipes around fictional strains and forms—smoked products, edibles, extracts, and hemp materials—with different effects on bloodlust, stamina, perception, consciousness, decay, or mutations. For example, a calming product could reduce bloodlust temporarily, while a different strain offers a short-lived survival benefit. The notes specify some effects but not a full ingredient list or recipe quantities, so exact formulas still need to be decided; balance each benefit with limited supplies, duration, and possible trade-offs.


Crafting as an exploration incentive: Put different materials, recipes, workstations, and knowledge in different regions. Exploring a lab, farm, forest, or abandoned building can reveal new ingredients or recipes; NPCs and recovered memories can also teach crafting. Resource quality can vary by location, weather, time, and tools.


Crafting progression: Start with simple multi-use tools, then unlock better weapons, shelter, water systems, and workbenches. Recipes can be learned through discovery, experimentation, flashbacks, and NPCs; advanced items can require a station, a skill level, or a specific region’s materials.


Inventory and crafting: Use a weight limit and soft capacity limit so players have to choose what to bring. Overloading can reduce stamina and movement, while backpacks, storage, animal haulers, and certain upgrades increase carrying capacity. A quick-drop and “move to storage” option can keep inventory management from becoming tedious.


Repair and maintenance: Give tools and weapons durability that decreases with use, misuse, and harsh weather. Field repairs can be quick but partial; workbench repairs can restore more durability and require appropriate materials and skills. Higher-tier items can require better stations or specialist NPCs to fully repair.


Crafting and the economy: Use barter and reputation rather than a universal currency. Human markets can value clean water, medicine, ammunition, tools, and useful information; the conscious-undead network can trade in blood types, extracts, mutation knowledge, and rare recipes. Reputation affects prices, stock, and access, making resources valuable for both crafting and relationships.


Connect gathering to the story: Make some finds carry narrative meaning as well as practical value. ChroniX facilities can contain research and rare samples tied to the Euphoria outbreak; old homes and camps can hold personal belongings, notes, and clues about Phoenix’s past. Rare discoveries can unlock quests, recipes, or story revelations.


Tools and abilities: Let players start with hand-gathering, then use tools to improve access and yield. The Multi-Stick supports basic digging; its sharpened form can cut plants and help with fishing or hunting. Axes, knives, and improved digging tools can open up more efficient harvesting. Exact tool requirements for each resource—such as mining stone—still need to be assigned.


Gathering limits: Use a low starting carry capacity so players must choose between, for example, cannabis materials, medical supplies, and crafting components. Overloading should reduce stamina and movement, making an overfull pack a real danger. Backpacks, storage, and animal haulers offer ways to expand capacity later.


Randomized resources: Use a saved world seed and procedural placement to vary resource locations, loot, wildlife, and encounters between generated worlds. Keep story-critical items and clues authored or guaranteed, so randomness changes how players find them without blocking the narrative.


Gathering in dangerous zones: Make risk proportional to potential reward. A quarantined hospital, lab vault, or military outpost can contain rare materials or research but also stronger enemies and more chances to be detected. Noise from gathering and the time spent exposed should make players decide whether to keep searching or leave.


Weather and time of day: Weather can affect resource quality and plant growth; time of day can change which threats are active and how safe gathering feels. The notes specify that zombies may become more aggressive at night and survivors may be more active during the day. They don’t define resources that are available only at night, so that could be added selectively for wildlife, encounters, or rare finds.


Resource scarcity: Scarcity should make players prioritize, reuse, cultivate, and trade—not turn every outing into a grind. Renewable resources such as seeds and hemp can support long-term crafting, while scarce items like medicine, tools, and rare research samples create meaningful choices. The design also proposes barter and reputation-based trading rather than a universal currency.


Player actions and resource availability: Let clearing a horde, securing a safehouse, or helping a faction change what’s safe to gather in an area. The notes support persistent local ecosystems and faction reactions; a useful extension is to make looted stashes stay empty for a while and let cleared zones gradually repopulate or attract other groups.


Gathering-tool upgrades: Upgrade tools for better yield, durability, and access to new resource types. The Multi-Stick can progress from a basic branch to sharpened, fire-hardened, stone-tipped, or improved digging variants. Tool quality and crafting skill can further improve efficiency; exact yield bonuses can be tuned during gameplay testing.


Exploration and discovery: Reward exploration with distinct regional resources, hidden containers, buried supplies, and access to caves, mines, tunnels, bunkers, or research facilities. Safehouses, randomized building interiors, and high-risk points of interest give players reasons to search beyond the main route.


How zombies evolve: Zombies should become more challenging through behavior and encounter composition, not just bigger health bars. They can follow scent, investigate sounds, roam in groups, and grow more aggressive at night; the notes don’t yet specify a system where individual zombies permanently learn from past encounters.


NPC reactions to choices: Track trust, rivalry, loyalty, and relationship history. NPCs can remember whether Phoenix kept promises, endangered others, fed in front of them, or helped their group; those reactions can change dialogue, quests, and whether they become allies or betray her.


Factions’ AI: Let factions patrol, trade, recruit, defend settlements, raid, and contest territory. Their activity can open or close routes and quests, so supporting one group changes the situation for the others—not just the player’s reputation score.


Quests, dialogue, and guidance: Give NPCs distinct backgrounds, routines, and personal quests. Their trust in Phoenix can unlock new dialogue, information, or guidance, while discoveries from memories and locations can introduce follow-up objectives.


Challenging zombie AI: Build encounters from different detection and response behaviors: zombies can see, hear, or smell Phoenix, investigate, search, chase, lose track, and return to patrol. Groups and night activity create pressure while leaving room for the player to hide or escape.


AI companions: Companions can follow, stay, explore, or return, with roles such as Guard, Scout, Hauler, or Companion. Outside combat they can help find resources or scout; in combat they can defend or warn Phoenix, while their health, hunger, and loyalty give players reasons to care for them.


Random NPC encounters: Use procedural encounters—patrols, lone survivors, wildlife, scavengers, and zombie groups—to make travel less predictable. Keep important character introductions and story beats authored so random generation doesn’t disrupt the main narrative.


NPCs adapting to player tactics: Add modest, readable adaptation: repeated noisy attacks may make enemies investigate sounds sooner, while overused routes may attract patrols. Signal the change and give the player counterplay; the current notes support configurable difficulty based on player progression and recent performance, but not detailed opponent learning.


Reactions to moral decisions: Avoid a simple “good/evil” meter. Let feeding, helping survivors, losing control, or siding with a faction affect separate reputations and relationships; that can change access to camps, dialogue, trading, and story resolutions.


AI-driven story events: Use horde attacks, faction raids, ambushes, and settlement events to change the player’s immediate priorities and reflect the changing world. Tie major events to story and faction conditions, with random events filling in the spaces between them. The design calls for local rules and weighted generation, so the core game needn’t rely on an external AI service.


9. Monetization & Replayability
Primary monetization: I recommend a paid base game, with optional cosmetic packs and substantial story or map expansions later. The notes list these as possible models and explicitly rule out pay-to-win and charging for core survival systems.


Progression and replayability: Different skill builds, blood mixtures, mutations, and character choices let players try different approaches on a new run. New Game+ can carry over selected discoveries or mutations while resetting story progress and world state.


Expansions or DLC: Additional regions, story chapters, characters, or quests fit the design, but no specific DLC roadmap is established yet. Prioritize a complete base-game arc before committing to expansions.


Choices and replayability: Branching relationships, faction loyalties, research decisions, and identity choices can lead to different endings. New Game+ can add dialogue that acknowledges previous major choices and endings.


Difficulty and challenge runs: Offer selectable modifiers such as No Cannabis, No Blood Mixing, Pacifist, Iron Phoenix/permadeath, and Flesh Only/no tools. Let players combine modifiers and earn titles or visual rewards for completing them.


Time-limited events: These aren’t specified in the notes. For a primarily single-player game, use optional seasonal scenarios or rotating challenges that can be replayed later, rather than time-limited rewards that punish players for missing a window.


Leaderboards and challenges: Challenge runs and achievements are already a better fit than competitive zombie-kill rankings. If leaderboards are added, make them opt-in and focus on speedruns, survival time, or challenge completion so they don’t reward disruptive play against story NPCs.


Cosmetic purchases: Keep paid items visual only—outfits, scars, tattoos, or alternate mutation appearances. Character looks and expression options can also be earned through story and challenges; don’t sell combat strength, resources, or survival advantages.


Mod support: The notes propose an exposed modding surface. Support community-made cosmetics, recipes, challenge modifiers, and—if technically practical—regions or quests, while keeping saves and core story content stable.


Randomized elements: Use a saved world seed to vary resource placements, wildlife, patrols, encounters, and minor locations between playthroughs. Keep story-critical locations and reveals consistent, so a new layout creates variety without making the narrative unreliable.


Soundtrack: Use sparse ambient music for exploration, with a dark, haunting palette that can turn tense or frightening. Lo-fi or cannabis-inspired textures could appear during specific moments or altered perceptions, but shouldn’t make the whole apocalypse feel lighthearted.


Atmosphere: Layer Phoenix’s movement with wind, weather, distant activity, and nonverbal zombie sounds. Use distance and environmental obstruction so a nearby moan feels different from one echoing across a street; spatially placed audio logs can also reward careful listening.


Dynamic music: Cross-fade between four states: calm exploration, tension, combat or chase, and uncontrolled horror. Increase percussion, drones, or melodic intensity as more enemies detect Phoenix or her bloodlust rises.


Voice acting: Phoenix’s voice can shift with her condition—more human when calm, rougher as bloodlust rises, and less verbal when uncontrolled. The notes also propose distinct voices for her inner perspectives and recognizable vocal signatures for conscious-undead characters. GanjaGuru isn’t identified in the current character notes; if that character is part of the cast, define their role and voice before choosing a performance style.


Ambient sounds: Make quiet moments informative: faint footsteps, rustling, distant voices, or zombie calls can suggest nearby danger. Weather and distance can obscure environmental sounds, encouraging players to pause, listen, and decide whether to move on.


Combat cues: Give attacks distinct, readable sounds—wind-up, impact, hit or miss, and enemy reaction—so players can respond even when the screen is crowded. Zombie sounds should convey detection, pursuit, or loss of target, complementing visual cues rather than replacing them.


Important music moments: Use short identity motifs for major mutations and story revelations, as proposed in the notes. Give major threats or boss-like encounters their own musical variation, but reserve those cues for genuinely significant moments so they stay memorable.


Cannabis audio feedback: Make consumption and strain effects audible as well as visible. The notes suggest that some strains or blood mixtures can temporarily alter Phoenix’s perception—for example, emphasizing nearby heartbeats or muting zombie sounds—while state changes should always receive clear feedback.


Stealth audio: Use footsteps, surface noise, and nearby enemy reactions to help players judge how exposed they are. A quiet floor, broken glass, and rain should sound and behave differently; suspicion cues should indicate when an enemy has heard something, without revealing its exact position automatically.


Overall soundscape: Let the contrast carry the tone: ruined-world ambience, body-horror vocal changes, moments of melancholy, and brief dry humor. Cannabis-related audio can express Phoenix’s perception and coping strategies without turning the soundtrack into a constant novelty cue.




1. **Connect gathering to the story:** Make some finds carry narrative meaning as well as practical value. ### **11. Character Creation & Identity**


1.Starting appearance: Players can customize Phoenix’s name, face shape, skin tone and zombie coloration, hair, eyes and eye condition, scars, tattoos, clothing, outfits, body proportions within the art style, and her initial decay appearance. Mature visual themes are mentioned, but the design doesn’t require explicit anatomy.


Backstory: The strongest direction is to keep Phoenix’s core history—her connection to ChroniX and the outbreak—fixed, while letting players customize parts of her past through background choices and memory fragments. That preserves the main story while giving players ownership over who she was before it.


Effects of starting background: Background choices can determine starting skills, location, early dialogue, and which memories or quest leads appear first. The notes list starting backgrounds, skills, and locations as customizable, but the exact effects still need to be defined.


Appearance changes over time: Decay should visibly affect Phoenix as it progresses, while permanent mutations can change her appearance and how NPCs react to her. Players can also choose an initial decay look; it should remain distinct from the actual decay meter, so appearance customization doesn’t secretly change difficulty.


Personality: Players can shape Phoenix’s personality through dialogue and actions. Those choices can influence her internal monologue and early conversations, while the story continues to respond to how she manages her human memories and zombie instincts.


Name, voice, and pronouns: A customizable character name is explicitly listed. Phoenix’s voice is designed to change with bloodlust, decay, and mutations, but player-selectable voice options and pronouns aren’t specified yet; they could be added as creator options if they fit the production scope.


Cosmetic versus gameplay choices: Keep face, hair, tattoos, and purely visual presets cosmetic. Starting skills, background, and location should affect gameplay. Clothing should be clearly identified as functional when it changes disguise or stealth, so players can tell when an outfit is more than appearance.


Unlockable appearance options: Players can unlock additional presets, outfits, clothing variants, scars, tattoos, and alternate visual styles for mutations through gameplay, story milestones, or challenges. The design calls for saving customization choices and unlocking additional cosmetic options as players progress.


### **12. Survival, Health & Failure**


Survival meters: The proposed meters are Health, Bloodlust, Decay, Stamina, Energy, Hydration, Body Temperature, Consciousness, Mental Stability, Human Suspicion, and Zombie Suspicion. They represent Phoenix’s physical condition, control, mental state, and risk of being detected; the design still needs to clarify how Energy, Consciousness, and Mental Stability differ mechanically.


Decay: As decay rises, it changes Phoenix’s appearance and can impair her movement and health, weaken her human disguise, and affect social interactions. Fictional treatments or cannabis effects can temporarily slow it.


Other survival needs: Yes. The design includes hydration, body temperature, and energy, plus a separate Fatigue meter. Fatigue rises with time awake, exertion, bloodlust, or injury; high fatigue reduces stamina and reaction speed, while weather affects temperature and movement.


Injuries and treatment: Phoenix can suffer bleeding, infection-related effects, broken limbs, movement impairment, and damage over time. Healing and recovery items are planned, but the specific treatments and recipes still need to be defined.


Dangerously high bloodlust: High bloodlust increases aggression, reduces control, makes disguises harder to maintain, and can trigger unwanted feeding. At 90 or above, it begins causing hunger damage; losing control in front of important NPCs can also seriously damage relationships or attract faction hostility.


Defeat and death: In standard mode, reaching zero health leads to a defeat screen where the player can load the last save or the last safehouse rest. The notes don’t impose extra progress loss beyond the player’s save timing. Optional Iron Phoenix permadeath mode makes death final for that save.


Safehouses: Safehouses can be secured and used to save, craft, and recover. A claimed safehouse supports a proper long rest; its security and comfort can improve rest, Sanity recovery, and bloodlust suppression. Some short rests are possible in hidden spots but provide fewer benefits and a greater chance of nightmares.


Difficulty and comfort settings: The proposed presets are Story, Normal, Hard, and Nightmare. Players can also adjust individual assists such as bloodlust and decay rates, resource yields, enemy detection, combat timing, aiming assistance, crafting hints, and quick-save limits. Endings are not locked behind difficulty or assists.




### **13. Blood Alchemy & Mutations**


1.Blood types and sources: Phoenix can collect human, zombie, and animal blood, including samples from dogs, cats, deer, wolves, rabbits, and rats. The notes also include rare Lab-Grade samples. Exact spawn locations aren’t mapped yet; a sensible placement is human blood around survivor areas, animal blood by species and biome, and Lab-Grade samples in ChroniX facilities.


Freshness and quality: Fresh samples produce stronger effects but spoil more quickly; stale blood is weaker. The design should make players weigh potency against the risk of carrying a sample until it degrades.


Discovering recipes: Players can learn mixes through experimentation, flashbacks, and NPCs. The proposed system lets players record successful combinations, mix up to three blood types at a workbench, or quick-mix two in the field.


Benefits and drawbacks: Mixtures can improve strength, stamina, regeneration, perception, night vision, speed, combat abilities, or decay resistance. Possible costs include instability, increased bloodlust, mutation side effects, weaker disguises, or temporary weakness.


Temporary and permanent effects: Most mixtures provide temporary effect packages; the design proposes a baseline duration of about 8–25 minutes. Repeated use can build “resonance” toward a permanent mutation, which changes Phoenix’s abilities and identity rather than simply expiring.


Mutation slots and changing choices: The design starts Phoenix with one permanent mutation slot and allows progression to expand the limit, up to four in one section. Mutations are intended to be lasting choices, not freely swappable; the notes don’t define a removal or respec system yet. One perk list also proposes an extra slot at high Blood Alchemy skill, so the final slot cap should be reconciled.


Effects on Phoenix: Mutations can change Phoenix’s appearance, combat options, dialogue, and disguise baseline. Human blood may make her appear and smell more human while increasing internal conflict; zombie or predator blood can make her stronger but more visibly undead.


NPC reactions: Humans can become more suspicious of visible mutations, while conscious undead may recognize and comment on recent mixtures. These reactions can affect dialogue, trust, disguise, and faction relationships, making a powerful build carry social consequences too.




### **14. Cannabis Cultivation & Processing**


Where to find seeds and strains: The design calls for seeds, wild plants, flowers, leaves, stems, hemp fiber, and distinct strains, but it doesn’t assign them to exact locations yet. A strong placement plan would put common seeds and plants in overgrown gardens, farms, and forest edges; rare or modified strains in greenhouses, faction caches, or ChroniX research sites.


Growth stages: Plants progress through six visible stages: seed, sprout, young plant, developing plant, mature plant, and harvestable plant.


Plant care: Players find or prepare dirt, plant seeds, water them, maintain growing conditions, then harvest and collect seeds. Exact light and soil-quality rules aren’t specified; sunlight outdoors and grow equipment indoors would give players useful choices.


Weather and seasons: Weather should affect growth and crop risk. The notes propose rain boosting growth but increasing mold risk, storms damaging exposed plants, heat increasing water needs, and cold slowing or stopping growth. Protected grow rooms and drying shelters can reduce those risks.


Products and uses: The proposed system includes smoking, edibles, and resin or extracts, as well as processing stems into hemp fiber. The specific recipe list isn’t finalized, so each product can be given a distinct use, duration, and resource cost.


Strain effects: Strains can differ in their effects on bloodlust, stamina, consciousness, perception, decay, and mutations. Make the effects readable in the Cannabis Journal, while keeping exact strain stats and recipe combinations to be balanced during testing.


Repeated use: The design mentions a “soft dependency,” but doesn’t define exact tolerance rules. A reasonable system would let repeated use of the same strain gradually reduce its benefit, while avoiding severe penalties that make the core survival loop feel punitive.


Hemp byproducts: Process stems into fiber, then use it for rope, cordage, fabric, clothing, fishing line, nets, bags, and shelter or building materials. That makes growing hemp useful even when Phoenix doesn’t need a bloodlust-management product


### **15. Building, Territory & Transportation**
Building progression: Start with a simple lean-to, then progress to sturdier huts, sheds, tents, or treehouses, and eventually convert caves or underground areas into shelters. The notes establish the range of shelter types but leave the exact progression and unlock requirements to be designed.


Placement: Players should build on valid terrain and be able to convert suitable caves or existing locations into shelters. The placement rules—such as terrain requirements, build radius, and restrictions near roads or story locations—aren’t specified yet.


Base stations: Proposed facilities include storage, workbenches, grow beds, drying racks, animal pens, and a bicycle workshop. These give a base practical roles in crafting, farming, animal care, and transportation.


Safehouse security: Players can secure locations by barricading doors, setting traps, and upgrading security. The design proposes that a fully claimed safehouse reaching Security Level 2 becomes permanent, though the exact defenses and upgrade costs still need definition.


Faction territory: Faction territory and pressure are intended to affect behavior, and the game includes faction raids and dynamic events. Whether factions can directly claim, attack, or take over a player’s base isn’t fully specified; a good approach is to make the risk depend on local faction relations and the base’s security.


Base functions: Grow beds let players plant and tend crops; drying racks process harvests; storage holds resources; workbenches enable more complex crafting; and animal pens provide shelter for companions. Comfort and security can also improve resting and recovery at a claimed safehouse.


Transportation: The concept includes bicycles, motorized bicycles, scooters, carts and trailers, as well as water travel from floating logs to crafted rafts. The exact parts and recipes for each vehicle still need to be set.


Effect on exploration and hauling: Land vehicles can help Phoenix travel between areas and carry supplies; carts and trailers make resource trips more efficient. Rafts let her cross rivers and lakes, explore shorelines, and transport materials across water, opening routes that aren’t accessible on foot.


### **16. Animal Companions & Relationships**


Animals: The proposed companions include rats, rabbits, dogs, cats, wolves, deer, birds, and other wildlife. Some, such as horses, cattle, or sheep, are also mentioned as possible tameable animals, but the final roster still needs to be chosen.


Earning trust: Players approach carefully, offer food, and build trust over repeated interactions. Phoenix’s bloodlust and disguise can affect how an animal reacts, so calming down or masking her scent may be important before approaching.


Companion roles: Assign animals roles such as Guard, Scout, Hauler, Companion, or Mount. For example, dogs can track and find resources, birds can scout or warn of danger, and haulers can increase carrying capacity.


Bloodlust, combat, and disguise: Companions should respond to Phoenix’s state and the danger around them. The notes propose animal temperaments and memory, and skills that can reduce animal fear of bloodlust; the exact combat and disguise rules still need to be set.


Companion needs: Animals have health, hunger, shelter, and loyalty needs. Players can provide care, train them, assign commands, and build animal pens to support them at a base.


Injury or loss: Companions can face danger, and their death or betrayal is intended to carry lasting consequences. The notes don’t define whether an animal can be recovered, replaced, or permanently lost, so that rule still needs to be decided.


Human and conscious-undead relationships: Develop relationships through trust, affinity, dialogue, shared events, and character-specific quests. NPCs have backgrounds, routines, and memories, so how Phoenix behaves can influence whether they become allies, rivals, or companions.


Support without romance: Yes. Romance is optional; friendship and trust can still provide meaningful support through quests, information, trading, faction access, or companionship


### **17. Quests, Progression & Narrative Delivery**


1Quest types: Main quests advance Phoenix’s core story and reveal the outbreak; side quests develop characters and factions; spontaneous events—such as raids, patrol encounters, or horde movements—create immediate situations in the world.


Quest sources: Survivors, faction members, companions, and conscious undead can offer quests. Players can also discover objectives through locations, research terminals, audio logs, and recovered memories.


Consequences: Record significant decisions as story flags. Helping or harming a character, earning a faction’s trust, or choosing what to do with ChroniX research can change later dialogue, mission options, and relationships.


Quest failure or expiry: The notes don’t set expiration rules. A fair approach is to keep main quests available, while allowing some optional quests to change or disappear when the world changes—clearly signaling when a decision will close off an opportunity.


Tracking objectives: Use a quest journal for current goals and discovered clues, a map for known points of interest, and dialogue reminders from relevant NPCs. Memory discoveries can add journal entries or unlock new quests.


Investigation and puzzles: Make investigation a recurring part of the story: connect evidence from locations, documents, recordings, and NPC conversations. Keep puzzles grounded in the environment and survival systems, with optional hints so a difficult puzzle doesn’t block the whole game.


Story versus exploration: Use main-quest milestones to guide progression and unlock regions, while leaving side quests, resources, and discoveries open to the player’s pace. The notes describe a semi-open world with optional survivor quests, safehouses, and high-risk locations.


Ending conditions: Base endings on the player’s accumulated choices: how Phoenix handles her identity and bloodlust, which factions and characters she supports, and what she does with the outbreak research. Possible outcome directions include restoration, embracing the undead, human–zombie coexistence, control of the outbreak, or isolation; the final ending list should be consolidated from the design notes.


### **18. Player Experience, Accessibility & Production**


HUD: Keep Bloodlust, Decay, disguise strength, and quick-use slots visible. Show stamina during exertion or combat, sanity when it becomes relevant, and noise or enemy-detection indicators during stealth. This keeps the screen uncluttered when Phoenix is safe, but informative when she’s threatened.


Quick access: Use a hotbar for commonly used items and a radial wheel for cannabis, blood, and medical items. Let players favorite items for the wheel; inventory, crafting, and character screens can hold the deeper management options.


Tutorial: Teach mechanics through the opening sequence, one at a time: movement and interaction, sneaking and sound, avoiding detection, first combat, gathering and crafting, then planting and using cannabis. Give players a chance to practice each action before adding another threat.


Accessibility: Planned options include remappable controls, text scaling, colorblind modes, screen-reader support, and combat assists. Add adjustable audio levels and captions for important sounds so stealth and story cues aren’t audio-only; those specific audio options aren’t detailed in the notes yet.


Saves: The design proposes multiple manual slots, autosave, quicksave, and a versioned save system. Saves should preserve the player’s inventory, skills, quests, relationships, claimed safehouses, crops, and world state; exact autosave timing and rollback rules still need to be set.


Platforms and controls: The notes list desktop, laptop, tablet, and mobile, with keyboard controls and a virtual joystick/touch buttons. They also mention PC, browser, and Android as possible targets, but the first launch platform hasn’t been settled—and the drafts differ on engine and target—so choose one platform to prioritize before promising all of them.


First playable prototype: The proposed vertical slice includes one region; movement and climbing; Bloodlust, Decay, and Stamina; basic cannabis use; a few blood mixtures; the Multi-Stick; basic disguise; two or three enemy types; crafting, shelter, inventory, saving, the opening sequence, and a day/night cycle.


Playtesting: The notes call for alpha testing and iteration but don’t define measures. For this prototype, check whether new players can understand the objective and key meters, complete the gather–craft–survive loop without outside help, recognize when they’ve been detected, and describe whether the tension feels engaging rather than confusing or unfair. Track where they get stuck and ask which moments they found memorable or frustrating.


### **19. Optional Multiplayer & Co-op**


Single-player or co-op: Make the main game single-player first. Treat co-op as optional future development so Phoenix’s story and survival loop work on their own.
Playable characters: Keep Phoenix as the protagonist; if co-op is added, let a second player create a separate conscious undead character rather than duplicate Phoenix.
Co-op activities: Support shared exploration, combat, gathering, crafting, and base-building.
Bloodlust and disguise: Track each player’s bloodlust and disguise separately. If one player is detected, nearby enemies can notice the group, so teammates have a reason to coordinate.
Relationship consequences: Make each player’s actions affect their own relationships and reputation. Shared faction consequences should apply only when the group is involved or witnessed.
Shared story choices: Use shared quest progress and a party decision for major story choices, with a clear confirmation before irreversible outcomes.
Joining and leaving: Allow drop-in/drop-out during exploration. Pause joining during major story scenes; if someone leaves, their character can wait safely or be controlled by the game.
Solo play: Keep the entire story playable alone. Co-op should add company, not become necessary to finish the game.
28. Health, Injuries & Recovery
Damage and injuries: Phoenix can suffer bleeding, infection-related effects, broken limbs, movement impairment, and damage over time.
Effects on play: Injuries should affect movement or other actions—for example, a broken leg could limit sprinting or climbing. The exact penalties still need to be defined.
Recovery: Use recovery items and healing, with claimed safehouses as places for proper rest. The notes establish recovery systems but don’t specify every treatment or recipe.
At zero health: Standard mode offers loading the last save or last safehouse rest. Optional Iron Phoenix mode makes death final for that save.
29. Survival Needs
Needs beyond health and bloodlust: The planned meters include Decay, Stamina, Energy, Hydration, Body Temperature, Consciousness, and Mental Stability. Human and Zombie Suspicion are also tracked, though they represent detection risk rather than a physical need.
How quickly they change: Bloodlust rises over time and Decay increases gradually; Fatigue builds with time awake, exertion, high bloodlust, or injury. Exact rates for the other meters aren’t specified and should be tuned with difficulty settings.
Critical-level penalties: High Decay affects appearance, disguise, movement, health, and social interactions. High Fatigue reduces stamina and reaction speed. Specific critical penalties for Hydration, Temperature, and Energy still need to be designed.
Recovery: Use rest and recovery items, with water and temperature-protective clothing or shelter addressing hydration and cold. Safehouse long rests can restore meters and support recovery; exact items and rates remain to be set.
30. Bloodlust & Control
What raises or lowers it: Bloodlust rises over time and faster near humans or wounded creatures, during combat, when hungry, and in some weather or nighttime conditions. Feeding, cannabis, and calming activities can reduce or suppress it.
State changes: Calm keeps Phoenix lucid and stealthy; Hungry makes her more aggressive and physically stronger; Uncontrolled lets zombie instincts take over, potentially making her attack friend and foe alike.
Regaining control: Use cannabis-based items, feed, or take calming actions. Bloodlust-control skills can extend suppression or help Phoenix regain control during chaotic situations.
Lasting consequences: Losing control can harm or destroy relationships with key characters; witnesses may spread word and raise hostility with factions. At very high bloodlust, hunger damage also begins.


### **31. Decay & Mental State**


1. **What worsens Phoenix’s condition:** Decay rises gradually. Fatigue increases with time awake, exertion, high bloodlust, and injury; mental stability can also be affected by high bloodlust, decay, nightmares, and feeding choices. 
2. **Effects on perception and abilities:** High decay affects appearance, movement, health, human disguise, and social interactions. Low mental stability can make internal monologue and sensory information less reliable, with possible audio or visual distortions. 
3. **Slowing or recovering:** Fictional treatments and cannabis effects can temporarily slow decay. A secure safehouse rest can restore meters and support recovery; sleep may trigger restorative dreams or nightmares depending on Phoenix’s state and recent choices. 
4. **Communicating the condition:** Show visible changes in Phoenix’s decay and use voice processing that shifts with bloodlust, decay, and mutations. Audio and visual feedback should signal important state changes so the player can tell when her condition has changed. 


### **32. Stealth & Disguise**


1. **Detection factors:** Humans and zombies evaluate Phoenix through sight, sound, and scent. Separate human and zombie suspicion and disguise values so fooling one group doesn’t automatically fool the other. 
2. **Different disguise layers:** Clothing and human-like movement can help with humans; rotten meat, zombie blood, damaged clothing, and mimicked movement can help with zombies. Noise and scent masking affect separate parts of detection.
3. **What exposes her:** Suspicious movement, sound, visible decay, blood, feeding, and attacking can raise human suspicion. Being too clean, smelling human, or moving unnaturally can raise zombie suspicion. Rain can also wash away an applied disguise. 
4. **Warnings and consequences:** Use suspicion indicators, noise and visibility cues, and NPC behavior—such as turning to investigate—to warn the player. If suspicion escalates, enemies can search, chase, or attack; a broken disguise should have both visual and audio confirmation. 


### **33. Combat**


1. **Starting and unlocked attacks:** Start with biting, scratching, and brute strength. Progress to punches, kicks, improvised weapons, spears, bows, thrown objects, and special zombie abilities. 
2. **How combat systems interact:** Attacks use cooldowns and stamina; weapons have durability. Bloodlust states alter Phoenix’s aggression and control, while biting can increase bloodlust and leave a disguise penalty—even if feeding later reduces bloodlust. 
3. **Non-combat options:** Players can sneak, hide, break line of sight, create distractions, use environmental cover, or escape with mobility abilities. Companions and tools can also give players alternatives to fighting head-on.
4. **Combat feedback:** Use clear attack animations, hit detection, knockback, enemy reactions, sounds, and visible health or damage states. Telegraph dangerous attacks so players can react rather than guess. 


### **34. Enemy AI & Encounters**


1. **Different enemy behaviors:** The proposed types include Walkers, Runners, Brutes, Spitters, and Lab Mutants, alongside human patrols, scavengers, and wildlife. Give each type a distinct threat and response pattern. 
2. **Detection and pursuit:** Enemies use vision range and cones, sound radius, scent detection, suspicion timers, searching, chasing, lost-target behavior, and returning to patrol. 
3. **Group behavior:** Zombies can roam in groups, follow scent, and follow zombie groups; humans can form groups, guard settlements, investigate suspicious activity, flee, or fight. 
4. **Adapting to the player:** The design calls for encounter difficulty to respond to player progression and recent performance. More specific learning—such as enemies reacting to repeatedly used tactics—isn’t defined yet and should remain readable and fair if added. 


### **35. Gathering**


1. **Resources and tools:** Players can gather branches, wood, stone, flint, grass, hemp, seeds, fiber, vines, clay, bones, hides, scrap metal, fabric, and more. Some resources can be collected by hand; tools improve access or efficiency. 
2. **Quality and yield:** Gathering quality can vary by biome, weather, time of day, and tools. This gives players a reason to seek the right region or conditions for a resource. 
3. **Replenishment:** Crops and wildlife are part of a persistent world simulation, but the exact respawn or depletion rules for resource nodes aren’t settled. Define which resources renew naturally, which must be cultivated, and which are finite.
4. **Gathering risks:** Scavenging can expose players to zombie ambushes, traps, rival survivors, and noise that attracts threats. Riskier locations can offer rarer materials or loot. 


### **36. Inventory & Equipment**


1. **Organization:** Use categories for tools, weapons, materials, food, blood, cannabis, seeds, clothing, medicine, and quest items. Support stacking, splitting, equipment slots, sorting, and a quick-access hotbar. 
2. **Capacity:** Use both weight and soft slot limits. Backpacks, certain mutations, hauler companions, and temporary blood mixtures can increase carrying capacity. 
3. **Overloading:** At over 70% capacity, stamina penalties begin; above 100%, movement slows and sprinting or climbing is disabled; above 130%, movement becomes very slow and Phoenix may be forced to drop items. 
4. **Showing item condition and use:** Display durability and visual wear for tools and clothing, alongside item descriptions and crafting relevance. Warn players when an item is close to breaking or when they’re nearing an encumbrance threshold. 


### **37. Crafting & Repair**


1. **Where crafting happens:** Simple recipes can be made in the field; more complex crafting uses workbenches or other stations. The design calls for stations in safe zones and safehouses. 
2. **Recipe rules:** Each recipe should show ingredients, crafting time, location requirements, output quantity, durability, skill level, and discovery status. Ingredients are consumed only after a successful craft. 
3. **Quality and upgrades:** Items can progress through Crude, Standard, Refined, Masterwork, and Legendary quality; higher quality improves durability and performance. Crafting skills can unlock advanced variants or secondary properties. 
4. **Repair:** Field repairs are quick and partial, using basic materials and slightly reducing maximum durability. Workbench repairs can fully restore items; top-tier gear may require a high-tier station or specialist NPC. 


### **38. Cannabis Cultivation & Use**


1. **Finding and growing plants:** Players find seeds or wild plants, prepare dirt, plant, water, maintain conditions, and harvest. Plants move through visible stages from seed to harvestable growth. 
2. **Growth conditions:** Weather affects growth and crop risk: rain can help growth but increase mold risk, storms can damage exposed plants, heat increases water needs, and cold can slow or stop growth. Protected grow rooms and drying shelters can reduce those risks. 
3. **Strain effects:** Strains can vary in their effects on bloodlust, stamina, consciousness, perception, decay, and mutations. Products can include smoked forms, edibles, and extracts, with hemp stems processed into fiber. 
4. **Tolerance and repeated use:** The notes mention a “soft dependency,” but don’t specify exact tolerance rules. Those rules still need balancing; one defined example is smoking reducing bloodlust by 30 points and stopping its rise for 25 seconds in the initial playable version. 


### **39. Blood Alchemy & Mutations**


1. **Collecting and storing blood:** Blood can come from humans, zombies, and wildlife such as dogs, cats, deer, wolves, rabbits, and rats. Store types separately; freshness matters because fresh samples have stronger effects but spoil faster. 
2. **Creating recipes:** Players can mix up to three blood types at a workbench, or two in a quick field mix. A discovery system records successful combinations; a stabilizer can extend or change effects. 
3. **Effects and risks:** Mixtures can change strength, stamina, regeneration, perception, night vision, speed, decay, or combat ability. Risks include instability, increased bloodlust, mutation side effects, reduced disguise, or temporary weakness; the proposed baseline effect duration is about 8–25 minutes. 
4. **Permanent mutations:** Phoenix starts with one permanent mutation slot, expandable through progression; one design section sets a maximum of four. Mutations are intended to be lasting choices, but the exact respec or removal rules—and the slot bonus in the Master Blood Alchemy perk—need reconciliation. 


### **40. Building & Safehouses**


1. **Structures and stations:** Players can build shelters ranging from lean-tos and huts to treehouses or converted caves, plus storage, workbenches, grow beds, drying racks, animal pens, and workshops. 
2. **Materials and placement:** Construction uses gathered resources such as wood, stone, fiber, rope, and other materials. Structures can be placed on suitable terrain or in usable existing locations; exact placement limits and recipes still need to be specified. 
3. **Security and maintenance:** Players can barricade doors, set traps, and upgrade security. Safehouses support saving, crafting, and recovery; comfort and security can improve rest and recovery. 
4. **Persistence and vulnerability:** A fully claimed safehouse at Security Level 2 becomes permanent; player-built structures aren’t removed unless dismantled or targeted by a major story event. The exact ways factions or world events can threaten a base still need defining.

### **41. Animal Companions**


1. **Finding and taming:** Players discover animals in the world, approach carefully, offer food, and build trust through repeated interactions. Taming skills can help animals calm faster or make higher-tier species approachable. 
2. **Roles and commands:** Companions can be assigned roles such as Guard, Scout, Hauler, Companion, or Mount, with commands to follow, stay, explore, and return. 
3. **Needs and danger:** Companions have health, hunger, and loyalty needs. Their temperament and memory affect how they react to Phoenix and danger, so players need to care for and shelter them. 
4. **Loss or injury:** Companion death or betrayal is intended to have lasting consequences. Exact recovery, replacement, and permanent-loss rules still need to be decided. 


### **42. NPC Relationships & Factions**


1. **Values tracked:** Track individual friendship, trust, rivalry, affinity, faction loyalty, and relationship history where relevant. Keep NPC reputation distinct from broader faction reputation. 
2. **What changes relationships:** Dialogue, quest outcomes, keeping promises, feeding, protecting people, or losing control can build trust or cause lasting conflict. NPCs are meant to remember significant events. 
3. **Faction effects:** Factions can respond by changing patrols, territory, quests, trade, raids, and hostility based on reputation and player actions. 
4. **Persistent hostility:** Severe negative reputation can block access to camps or services and provoke hostile reactions. Some relationship damage from losing control in front of key NPCs can be permanent. 


### **43. Quests & Story Flags**


1. **Quest flow:** Players discover quests through NPCs, factions, locations, and memory clues; track them in a journal; then complete or fail them based on objectives and choices. The exact failure rules still need definition. 
2. **Lasting flags:** Record major decisions such as whom Phoenix helps or harms, faction loyalties, key relationship changes, and what she does with ChroniX research. These flags can alter later missions and dialogue. 
3. **Missable quests or endings:** Optional quests can become unavailable as the world changes, but the notes don’t establish whether major endings can be missed. I recommend keeping every major ending reachable through a clear chain of choices rather than a single easy-to-miss side quest.
4. **Rewards:** Quests can award resources, recipes, skills, allies, reputation, information, and new story routes—so players can choose rewards that suit their playstyle. 


### **44. Skills & Progression**


1. **Earning progression:** Players earn skill points through use, quests, and story milestones; the notes propose three tiers—Novice, Adept, and Master—for seven skill categories. 
2. **Improving skills:** Some skills improve through repeated use, while major perks require spending earned points. The design includes skills such as Stealth, Combat, Herbalism, Blood Alchemy, Animal Handling, Crafting, and Social. 
3. **Unlocking approaches:** Skills can open new abilities, dialogue, recipes, taming options, or stealth and combat tactics, supporting different character builds. 
4. **Respec:** The notes don’t define skill respecs. A reasonable approach is to allow limited respec at a safehouse or specialist, while keeping permanent mutations as more consequential choices.


### **45. World Simulation, Weather & Events**


1. **Changes over time:** Crops, animals, and regional pressure are intended to persist and respond to player actions. How much the game simulates while an area is off-screen still needs specification. 
2. **Weather and time:** Weather can affect visibility, sound, movement, plant growth, resource availability, stealth, and animal behavior. Time of day changes enemy aggression, patrols, wildlife, and settlement activity. 
3. **Scripted and generated events:** Use authored events for key story moments and procedurally selected encounters, patrols, resource locations, and faction events for variety. 
4. **Player impact:** Actions can shift local ecosystems and faction behavior. Clearing areas, building a base, or supporting a faction should change local pressure; the exact pace of population recovery and resource replenishment remains to be set. 


### **46. Economy & Trading**


1. **Faction preferences:** Human markets value clean water, medicine, ammunition, tools, information, and high-quality cannabis; the conscious-undead network values blood, extracts, mutation knowledge, and rare recipes. 
2. **Reputation and appearance:** Human traders may distrust visible mutations or evidence of feeding, while conscious undead are more accepting. Reputation affects prices, available stock, dialogue, and access to trading locations. 
3. **Scarcity:** Make common resources renewable or recoverable, while rare materials and research remain valuable. The exact supply and restock rates for important goods still need balancing.
4. **What can be traded:** The proposed economy uses barter and reputation instead of universal currency. Players can trade resources, crafted goods, rare samples, and information. 


### **47. UI, Saves & Accessibility**


1. **HUD versus menus:** Keep Bloodlust, Decay, disguise strength, and quick-use slots visible; show stamina, sanity, noise, and detection cues contextually. Put deeper systems—blood mixing, cannabis strains, identity, relationships—in dedicated screens. 
2. **Tutorials and feedback:** Teach systems through the opening sequence, then use contextual prompts and clear audio/visual signals for state changes, discoveries, and danger. 
3. **Save data:** The design proposes multiple manual slots, autosave, quicksave, and versioned saves. Save inventory, skills, quests, relationships, world seed, buildings, crops, and other persistent world state; exact save timing still needs a decision. 
4. **Options:** Planned accessibility includes remappable controls, text scaling, colorblind modes, screen-reader support, and combat assists. Difficulty can also be adjusted through granular options for bloodlust, decay, resources, detection, and combat timing. 


### **48. Optional Multiplayer & Co-op**


1. **Single-player or multiplayer:** Keep the core game single-player; co-op is not defined in the current design and should be treated as optional future scope.
2. **What is shared:** If co-op is added, share exploration, base-building, and main quest progress, while keeping each player’s inventory, skills, and character condition separate.
3. **Group consequences:** Track bloodlust and disguise per player, but let one player’s detection put nearby teammates at risk. Apply faction consequences individually unless the whole group is seen taking an action.
4. **Solo completion:** Keep the full story playable alone, with co-op adding company rather than required abilities or story access.
