The attachment contains five sets of questions. Here are answers for all of them; where the design notes don’t specify an exact rule, I’ve marked it as a decision to finalize.


### **Crafting System**


1. **Opening recipes:** Start with a basic Multi-Stick and a sharpened stick, then let players craft simple tools, weapons, and survival items. The sharpened stick’s listed recipe is one branch and one stone. 
2. **Field vs. station crafting:** Simple recipes can be made in the field; complex recipes require a workbench or another suitable station, often in a secured area.
3. **Recipe discovery:** Players find recipes through exploration and experimentation, flashbacks, and NPCs. Successful recipes are recorded for later use. 
4. **Recipe requirements:** Each recipe should specify ingredients, time, output, durability, skill requirement, and whether a station is needed. Exact ingredient quantities and crafting times still need to be set recipe by recipe.
5. **Quality and skill:** Crafting skills can improve speed, reduce material waste, unlock quality tiers, and allow secondary item properties. Higher-quality items should generally perform better and last longer. 
6. **Upgrades:** Upgrade tools and weapons through improved materials and recipe variants—for example, sharpening or reinforcing a Multi-Stick. Clothing and shelter can also gain better protection or function as part of a disguise.
7. **Repair and salvage:** Field repairs are quick but partial; workbench repairs can fully restore equipment. Some broken items can be salvaged, while the Multi-Stick can be reshaped into a basic stick. 
8. **Regional progression:** New regions introduce materials, recipes, stations, and knowledge that support more advanced crafting. Rare areas such as labs can supply specialized research or components.
9. **What crafting supports:** Recipes should serve survival, combat, stealth, farming, building, and travel—such as water filters, weapons, disguises, grow equipment, and transport components.
10. **Crafting interface:** Show ingredients, missing items, time, skill and station requirements, output, and durability before crafting. For mixtures, include a preview of effects and risks.


### **Gathering System**


1. **Resources and locations:** Gather wood, stone, flint, plants, seeds, hemp, fiber, bones, hides, scrap metal, fabric, and containers. Their locations should reflect the biome: plants and seeds in overgrown areas, scrap in ruins, and wildlife resources where those species live. 
2. **Tool requirements:** Some materials can be collected by hand; tools improve access or efficiency. The Multi-Stick supports digging, fishing, hunting, and plant-cutting, while improved tools should unlock or speed up more demanding gathering.
3. **Biome and conditions:** Resource quality and yield can vary by biome, weather, time, and tools. Seasonal and weather-specific yield rules still need tuning. 
4. **Time and danger:** Gathering takes time and can expose Phoenix or create noise that attracts threats. Risk should increase in high-threat areas and when she gathers carelessly.
5. **Replenishment:** Crops and wildlife are part of the persistent world simulation, but exact resource-node respawn rules aren’t defined. Decide which resources regrow, which must be cultivated, and which are finite.
6. **Story-linked finds:** Put rare resources, seeds, research, or samples in places tied to ChroniX, the outbreak, and Phoenix’s memories. High-risk sites can reward players with valuable materials or clues.
7. **Capacity:** Use a hybrid weight-and-slot inventory. Low initial capacity forces players to choose what to take; backpacks, mutations, and hauler companions can expand it. 
8. **Risk and reward:** Dangerous zones—such as a quarantined hospital, collapsed metro, or lab vault—can contain rare loot but also stronger enemies and greater exposure risk. 
9. **Gathering upgrades:** Herbalism and tool skills can improve harvest speed, quality identification, and access to rare plants; upgraded tools can improve efficiency or durability.
10. **Signaling resources:** Use distinctive silhouettes, environmental clues, map discoveries, and optional sensory abilities to help players notice resources without making every useful item glow.


### **Combat System**


1. **Starting attacks:** Phoenix begins with bites, scratches, and brute force; she can later unlock punches, kicks, improvised weapons, spears, bows, thrown objects, and special zombie abilities. 
2. **Melee and ranged:** Melee is the core because Phoenix is a zombie, while bows and thrown objects give her limited ranged options. They let her damage or distract enemies before closing in; firearms aren’t fully specified in the current design.
3. **Bloodlust and stamina:** Combat and nearby wounded creatures can raise bloodlust, while attacks consume stamina. Hungry Phoenix becomes more aggressive and powerful, but Uncontrolled Phoenix risks attacking friend and foe alike. 
4. **Enemy tactics:** Runners pressure the player’s space, Brutes demand repositioning, and Spitters threaten from range; human patrols and scavengers use different tactics from zombies. Exact attack kits for these types still need definition.
5. **Using the environment:** Players can use cover, hiding places, distractions, traps, and escape routes. Human NPCs can set traps, creating hazards that may also be used to redirect enemies. 
6. **Noise and disguise:** Attacking, biting, and feeding can draw attention; biting also creates a gore/disguise penalty. A noisy fight may attract other threats. 
7. **Disengaging:** Players can sneak away, hide, break line of sight, climb, dash, or use distractions rather than fight every enemy.
8. **Rewards:** Combat can provide loot and resources, while experience, quests, and progression milestones can unlock skills or abilities. Rewards should not make combat the only viable way to progress.
9. **Difficulty:** Adjust encounters based on player progression and recent performance, changing enemy mix and pressure without making encounters unfair. 
10. **Encounter flow:** Build each encounter around warning, decision, action, and aftermath: hear or spot a threat, choose stealth or combat, resolve the danger, then manage injuries, noise, loot, and bloodlust.


### **Story System**


1. **Main phases:** Organize the story into three broad acts: Phoenix awakens and survives; investigates ChroniX and grows into her abilities; then chooses the future she wants. The notes also describe more detailed milestones within those acts. 
2. **Plot-driving events:** Key events include escaping ChroniX, discovering the outbreak’s cause, meeting other conscious undead and former colleagues, and deciding what to do with the research.
3. **Main, side, and optional content:** Main quests advance the outbreak mystery; side quests develop survivors and factions; optional discoveries add resources, memories, and lore.
4. **Choices:** Decisions about feeding, helping or harming survivors, faction support, and the ChroniX research can change relationships, quests, and endings. 
5. **Failing or abandoning quests:** The notes don’t define quest-failure rules. A fair default is to keep core story quests available while allowing some side quests to change when the player’s actions alter the world.
6. **Factions and companions:** Factions create competing goals and quest paths; companions and key NPCs can provide information, support, and relationship-specific story moments.
7. **Interactive versus cinematic scenes:** Use cinematics for key transitions, but keep discoveries, conversations, and memory sequences interactive when player choices or exploration matter.
8. **Tracking clues and memories:** Record discoveries in a journal and mark known locations on the map. Recovered memories can unlock journal entries, dialogue, quests, or abilities. 
9. **Ending conditions:** Base endings on accumulated choices: Phoenix’s approach to her identity and bloodlust, alliances and relationships, and the fate of the ChroniX research. The final list of endings should be consolidated from the design notes.
10. **Pacing:** Alternate survival and exploration with character scenes and major revelations. Let optional quests and discoveries fill out the world without making them mandatory for understanding the main plot.


### **Backstory System**


1. **Phoenix before the outbreak:** Phoenix was a scientist or engineer connected to ChroniX’s experimental biotechnology division, with access to its research and cannabis program. 
2. **Her role at ChroniX:** She was involved with the resurrection experiment and the Euphoria project. The exact job title and level of responsibility should be finalized.
3. **Cause of the outbreak:** The notes describe the Euphoria strain as contaminated with an experimental viral agent, which spread during a ChroniX event. Who knew the risk and when remains part of the mystery. 
4. **Why Phoenix is conscious:** The resurrection experiment returned her with continuity of consciousness, but why she retained it while most infected did not is a central mystery for players to uncover.
5. **People from her past:** Dr. Lena Voss is a former researcher, Marcus is a former EMT who becomes an important survivor, and Riven is another conscious undead character. Their exact histories and roles should be kept consistent across the final GDD. 
6. **Guilt and unfinished goals:** Phoenix’s connection to the research gives her personal stakes in learning what happened. The notes don’t settle exactly what she knew or did before the outbreak; that can shape her guilt, responsibility, or sense of complicity.
7. **Fixed versus customizable past:** Keep the ChroniX and outbreak connection as the story’s fixed backbone. Let players customize selected memories, personality leanings, and starting background to vary dialogue and early options. 
8. **Memory triggers:** Memories can surface at ChroniX locations, through photographs and documents, familiar voices, research terminals, former coworkers, and Euphoria discoveries. 
9. **Open mysteries:** Preserve questions about what happened at the ChroniX event, Dr. Thorne’s aims, why Phoenix is different, what happened to her coworkers, and whether a cure is possible. 
10. **How the truth changes her:** Learning the truth should shift Phoenix’s goals from immediate survival toward a chosen future: pursuing restoration, embracing her undead identity, or seeking coexistence with humans.

### **NSFW & Mature-Content System**


1. **Themes and boundaries:** Keep the game’s mature focus on horror, gore, dark humor, cannabis, and optional adult relationships. All romance should involve consenting adults; define any content limits before writing scenes. 
2. **Disabling content:** Yes. Let players disable romance and mature material in settings, ideally before starting.
3. **Global and per-scene settings:** Offer a global Off / Suggestive / Explicit setting, with a skip option for individual scenes. 
4. **Consent and boundaries:** Make consent and character boundaries clear in dialogue; violating a boundary should have a meaningful relationship consequence, not unlock intimacy.
5. **Completing the story without intimacy:** Yes. Romance and intimate content should never be required for main quests or endings. 
6. **Relationship consequences:** Relationship choices can affect trust, affinity, dialogue, and epilogues without requiring sexual content. Friendship and loyalty should also provide meaningful story support.
7. **What is shown:** Let the player choose whether scenes are explicit, suggestive, or off; use fade-to-black where appropriate.
8. **Settings’ effects:** The toggle should adjust dialogue, visuals, and animation consistently, without breaking quest progression or character relationships.
9. **Ratings and platforms:** Target an adult rating, provide clear content warnings and age gating, and check each storefront’s current rules before selecting platforms. The notes call out platform compliance as a requirement. 
10. **Coherence with content disabled:** Keep all essential information, character development, rewards, and endings available in the non-explicit version.


### **Stealth System**


1. **Detection:** Use three detection channels—sight, sound, and scent—with separate human and zombie suspicion values. 
2. **Two disguises:** Human disguise depends on looking, smelling, and behaving plausibly human; zombie disguise depends on masking human scent, wearing damaged clothing, and mimicking zombie behavior.
3. **Improving disguise:** Use clean clothing and careful behavior around humans; use zombie blood or scent-masking materials and zombie-like movement around undead. Disguise is temporary and must be maintained.
4. **Raising suspicion:** Unnatural movement, noise, visible decay, blood, feeding, or attacking can expose Phoenix to humans. Being too clean, smelling human, or acting unusually can attract zombies. 
5. **Cover and hiding:** Use shadows, bushes, hiding places, obstacles, and broken line of sight to avoid detection; distractions can redirect enemies.
6. **Weather and surfaces:** Rain can wash away an external disguise; fog reduces visual detection; surfaces such as gravel, glass, and metal make more noise than grass or dirt. 
7. **Suspicion stages:** Give enemies readable stages: unaware, suspicious, investigating, searching, and chasing. Use visual and audio cues to show escalation.
8. **Stealth failure:** If detected, enemies investigate or pursue; the player can fight, hide, flee, or break line of sight. Being caught should raise local danger and potentially affect faction relations.
9. **Silent takedowns:** Make stealth and avoidance the core. If quiet takedowns are added, keep them situational; feeding or leaving evidence should still risk discovery.
10. **Stealth skills:** Skills can reduce footstep noise and scent, speed up disguise application, extend disguise duration, and eventually allow brief periods of near-silent movement. 


### **Inventory System**


1. **Capacity:** Use both item slots and weight, so players face meaningful choices about what to carry. 
2. **Stacking:** Allow common materials and consumables to stack; set stack limits by item type and weight. Exact stack sizes still need balancing.
3. **Organization:** Separate tools, weapons, materials, food, blood, cannabis, seeds, clothing, medicine, quest items, and animal supplies into clear categories. 
4. **Quick access:** Provide four to six quick-use slots and a radial wheel for frequently used items; allow players to favorite items for the wheel. 
5. **Overload:** Apply increasing penalties as weight exceeds capacity: reduced stamina first, then slower movement and loss of sprinting or climbing, followed by forced item drops at extreme overload. 
6. **Spoilage and condition:** Track weapon and tool durability; blood freshness declines over time, while weather can affect disguise materials and crops. Define spoilage rules for other items separately.
7. **Storage and companions:** Let players transfer items to safehouse storage and suitable animal haulers. Show the destination and capacity before confirming a transfer.
8. **After defeat:** Standard mode returns the player to a save or safehouse rest; the notes don’t specify dropped-inventory loss. I recommend not adding a dropped-bag penalty unless it’s clearly communicated as a difficulty option. 
9. **Sorting and comparison:** Include search, sorting, item splitting, favorites, and side-by-side equipment comparisons. 
10. **Item information:** Show an item’s function, weight, condition, stack size, crafting uses, and any relevant effects or risks.


### **Building & Styling System**


1. **Shelters:** Let players start with a lean-to and progress to huts, tents, treehouses, or converted caves and underground shelters. 
2. **Placement:** Allow construction on suitable terrain and at appropriate locations; define restrictions for terrain, safehouse boundaries, and story-critical areas.
3. **Materials:** Use gathered wood, stone, fiber, rope, scrap, and other resources. Tools and stations can be required for more complex structures.
4. **Defenses:** Barricades and traps help secure a safehouse against threats; walls, doors, and security upgrades should make its level of protection clear.
5. **Base stations:** Include storage, workbenches, grow beds, drying racks, animal pens, and a bicycle workshop. 
6. **Decoration:** Let players place furniture, lighting, wall decorations, trophies, notes, grow equipment, and personal items.
7. **Functional decoration:** Some items should improve storage, comfort, drying, or recovery as well as appearance. A base’s comfort score can affect rest and mental recovery. 
8. **NPC and companion visits:** Companions can use animal pens; trusted NPCs may visit or comment on the base. Whether NPCs can permanently live there remains to be decided.
9. **Persistence:** Claimed safehouses become permanent at Security Level 2; player structures persist unless dismantled or targeted by a major story event. 
10. **Base development:** As it grows, a base can shift from a basic shelter into a specialized space—such as a greenhouse, fortress, workshop, or sanctuary—with corresponding functional and visual changes.


### **Character Creation & Customization**


1. **Customizable traits:** Allow players to customize name, face, skin and zombie coloration, hair, eyes, scars, tattoos, clothing, outfits, and stylized body proportions. 
2. **Fixed identity:** Keep Phoenix’s connection to ChroniX and the outbreak as the core story; let players customize selected memories, personality leanings, and starting background.
3. **Starting choices:** Starting skills, background, and location can shape early dialogue and options. Exact quest effects still need to be written.
4. **Decay and mutations:** Let decay visibly alter Phoenix over time; mutations can create lasting appearance changes and affect abilities or how NPCs perceive her. 
5. **Cosmetic versus functional:** Keep face, hair, and tattoos cosmetic; clearly label clothing or mutations that affect disguise, stats, or abilities.
6. **Changing appearance later:** Allow appearance edits at a safehouse mirror or character screen, while keeping story-related mutations and decay visible unless the player has a specific way to alter them.
7. **Unlockables:** Unlock outfits, appearance presets, scars, tattoos, and alternate visual styles through gameplay, story, or challenges. 
8. **NPC reactions:** NPCs may react to visible decay, mutations, and clothing; human and undead responses should differ.


### **Survival, Health & Recovery**


1. **Meters:** Track Health, Bloodlust, Decay, Stamina, Energy, Hydration, Body Temperature, Consciousness, and Mental Stability, plus separate human and zombie suspicion values. 
2. **What changes them:** Bloodlust rises over time and faster near humans, wounded creatures, in combat, and under certain conditions. Decay rises gradually; fatigue grows with time awake, exertion, high bloodlust, and injuries.
3. **Critical effects:** High decay affects appearance, movement, health, disguise, and social interactions. High fatigue lowers stamina and reaction speed; exact critical effects for hydration and temperature still need definition. 
4. **Injuries:** Include bleeding, infection-related effects, broken limbs, movement impairment, and damage over time.
5. **Healing:** Recovery items and healing treatments can restore health; fictional treatments and cannabis effects can temporarily slow decay. Exact item recipes remain to be set.
6. **Rest:** Secure safehouses support long rests and recovery; short rests in hidden spots provide fewer benefits and may carry more risk. 
7. **Defeat:** In standard mode, let players load the last save or last safehouse rest. Offer Iron Phoenix as an optional permadeath mode. 
8. **Difficulty settings:** Offer Story, Normal, Hard, and Nightmare presets plus individual assists for bloodlust and decay rates, resource yields, detection, combat timing, aim assist, crafting hints, and quick-save limits. Endings should remain available at every setting.

The attachment includes seven sets of questions. Here are answers to all of them; where the design notes don’t specify a rule, I’ve called that out.


### **Blood Alchemy & Mutations**


1. Phoenix can collect human, zombie, and animal blood, including samples from dogs, cats, deer, wolves, rabbits, and rats. The design also includes rare Lab-Grade samples. 
2. Fresh blood gives stronger effects but spoils faster; stale blood has weaker effects.
3. Players discover recipes through experimentation, NPCs, and story discoveries. A journal records successful mixes; the proposed system allows three blood types at a workbench or two in a quick field mix. 
4. Mixtures can boost strength, stamina, regeneration, perception, speed, night vision, or decay resistance. Risks include instability, increased bloodlust, mutation side effects, reduced disguise, or temporary weakness. 
5. Most mixtures create temporary effects; blood resonance can contribute toward permanent mutations that change abilities, appearance, and social reactions. 
6. Phoenix starts with one permanent mutation slot, expandable through progression; one design section sets four as the cap. Another perk proposes an extra slot, so the final limit needs reconciling. 
7. Mutations can unlock combat abilities, affect disguise baselines, change appearance, and alter NPC reactions. For example, human blood may improve human camouflage, while predator or zombie blood can make Phoenix more powerful but more visibly undead. 
8. Mutations are intended to be lasting choices; the notes don’t define a normal respec or removal system. New Game+ can let players keep or reset mutations, and discovered recipes are also listed as possible carry-over choices. 


### **Cannabis Cultivation & Processing**


1. Players can find seeds and wild plants while exploring; the notes also propose hidden strains and genetics. Specific guaranteed locations and seed sources still need to be mapped. 
2. Plants pass through seed, sprout, young plant, developing plant, mature plant, and harvestable stages. 
3. Players prepare dirt, plant seeds, water plants, maintain growing conditions, harvest flowers, collect seeds, and process stems.
4. Weather affects growth and crop risk: rain can help growth but raise mold risk; storms can damage exposed plants; heat increases water needs; cold slows or stops growth. Protected grow rooms and drying shelters can reduce those effects. 
5. Strains vary in quality and can affect bloodlust, stamina, consciousness, perception, decay, or mutations. Exact strain-by-strain effects still need balancing.
6. Products can include smoked forms, edibles, and extracts; stems can be processed into hemp fiber. 
7. The design mentions “soft dependency,” but doesn’t settle exact tolerance rules. A prototype effect is specified: smoking reduces bloodlust by 30 and pauses its increase for 25 seconds; repeated-use penalties need tuning. 
8. Hemp can become fiber, rope, cordage, fabric, clothing, fishing line, nets, bags, and shelter or building materials. 


### **Animal Companions**


1. Possible companions include dogs, cats, wolves, deer, rabbits, rats, birds, foxes, and other wildlife. The broader concept also mentions larger domestic animals; the final roster is not settled. 
2. Approach carefully, offer food, and build trust over repeated encounters. Animal Handling skills can help animals calm faster and enable taming more difficult species. 
3. Roles include Guard, Scout, Hauler, Companion, and Mount. Examples: dogs track and find resources; birds scout and warn; haulers increase carrying capacity. 
4. Companions can follow, stay, explore, and return. Additional commands can let them guard, scout, haul, or assist in combat.
5. Companions need food, shelter, health care, and loyalty management. 
6. Temperament, bloodlust, disguise, and mutations can affect animal reactions. The notes propose reduced animal fear from bloodlust as an Animal Handling perk.
7. Companions can be endangered; their death or betrayal is meant to carry lasting weight. Exact recovery, replacement, and permanent-loss rules need deciding. 
8. Yes. Players can train abilities, improve commands, build trust, and unlock stronger companion roles through Animal Handling progression. 


### **NPC Relationships & Factions**


1. Track NPC friendship, trust, rivalry, affinity, faction loyalty, and significant remembered events; use separate reputation values for factions. 
2. Keeping promises, helping, feeding, protecting, or losing control can raise or damage relationships. Betrayal and witnessed feeding can have lasting consequences.
3. NPCs should remember important choices and use that history to change dialogue, trust, and future interactions. 
4. Factions can respond with changed patrols, hostility, quests, trade, raids, recruitment, and settlement activity. 
5. Yes. Territory ownership and regional pressure are planned, though exact takeover and recovery rules need defining. 
6. Reputation can change quest access, dialogue, prices, stock, and access to faction encampments. 
7. NPCs can become allies, enemies, merchants, companions, or romance interests where appropriate. Romance is optional.
8. Losing a major NPC or becoming hostile to a faction can close off services, quests, and story paths. The exact consequences should be clear before irreversible choices where possible.


### **Quests, Progression & Endings**


1. Main quests advance the outbreak mystery; side quests develop NPCs and factions; optional discoveries add lore, resources, and recipes.
2. Players find quests through NPC dialogue, faction hubs, exploration, memory triggers, and research discoveries. The journal and map help track them. 
3. Rewards can include skills, recipes, allies, reputation, information, abilities, and story opportunities—not only items.
4. Choices can alter later dialogue, relationships, faction access, research outcomes, quests, and endings. 
5. Quest expiry and failure rules aren’t settled. A good default is to keep core story quests available while allowing some optional objectives to change as the world reacts.
6. Endings should depend on accumulated choices about Phoenix’s identity, bloodlust, alliances, relationships, and the ChroniX research—not a single isolated decision. The proposed outcomes include restoration, embracing the undead, coexistence, controlling or destroying ChroniX, and independent survival. 




The attachment includes seven sections, so I’ve answered all of them. Where the design notes leave a rule open, I’ve said so.


### **Blood Alchemy & Mutations**


1. **Blood types:** Phoenix can collect human, zombie, and animal blood, including from dogs, cats, deer, wolves, rabbits, and rats. Rare Lab-Grade samples are also proposed. 
2. **Freshness and quality:** Fresh blood has stronger effects but spoils faster. Mixtures can also vary by quality and stability. 
3. **Discovering recipes:** Players discover mixtures through experimentation, flashbacks, and NPCs. Successful combinations are recorded; players can mix up to three blood types at a workbench or two in the field. 
4. **Benefits and drawbacks:** Mixtures can improve strength, stamina, regeneration, perception, night vision, speed, decay resistance, or combat ability. They may also cause instability, higher bloodlust, mutation side effects, weaker disguise, or temporary weakness. 
5. **Temporary versus permanent:** Mixtures usually create temporary effects; blood “resonance” can also contribute toward permanent mutations. Proposed temporary effects last roughly 8–25 minutes, depending on the mix.
6. **Mutation limit:** The design starts Phoenix with one permanent mutation slot and proposes expanding it to four. A skill perk also mentions an extra slot, so the final maximum needs to be reconciled. 
7. **Effects on Phoenix:** Mutations can change appearance, combat options, dialogue, and disguise. Human blood can improve human camouflage, while zombie or predator blood can make Phoenix stronger but more visibly undead.
8. **Changing mutations and New Game+:** Mutations are intended to be lasting choices. New Game+ may let players keep or reset mutations, but the notes don’t specify how to remove or replace them during a normal playthrough. 


### **Cannabis Cultivation & Processing**


1. **Finding seeds:** Seeds and wild plants can be found while exploring; rare strains can be tied to hidden locations, research sites, or trading. Exact spawn locations still need to be mapped.
2. **Growth stages:** Plants progress through seed, sprout, young, developing, mature, and harvestable stages. 
3. **Plant care:** Players prepare dirt, plant seeds, water them, and maintain growing conditions. The notes don’t yet specify exact soil or lighting requirements.
4. **Weather, season, and location:** Rain can boost growth but create mold risk; storms can damage exposed plants; heat increases water needs; and cold can slow or stop growth. Protected grow rooms and drying shelters help manage conditions. 
5. **Strains, quality, and effects:** Strains can vary in quality and affect bloodlust, stamina, consciousness, perception, decay, or mutations. Players can identify and record strains in a journal. 
6. **Products:** Players can smoke cannabis, make edibles or extracts, and process stems into hemp fiber. The exact recipe list remains to be designed.
7. **Duration and tolerance:** One prototype effect is smoking cannabis to reduce bloodlust by 30 points and pause its rise for 25 seconds. The broader design mentions a “soft dependency,” but exact tolerance rules aren’t specified yet. 
8. **Hemp uses:** Hemp can become fiber, rope, cordage, fabric, clothing, fishing line, nets, bags, and shelter or building materials. 


### **Animal Companions**


1. **Companion species:** Proposed animals include dogs, cats, wolves, rabbits, rats, deer, and birds, with other species such as horses also under consideration. The launch roster still needs to be chosen.
2. **Taming:** Players approach carefully, offer food, and build trust over repeated interactions. Animal-handling skills can help animals calm faster.
3. **Roles:** Companions can Guard, Scout, Haul, accompany Phoenix, or serve as a Mount. Dogs can track; birds can warn of danger; haulers increase carrying capacity. 
4. **Commands:** Use clear commands such as follow, stay, explore, and return, with role-specific orders where appropriate. 
5. **Needs:** Companions have health, hunger, loyalty, and shelter needs; players can feed, train, and house them.
6. **Bloodlust and mutations:** Animals can react to Phoenix’s bloodlust, disguise, and behavior. The exact response should depend on species and temperament; animal-handling skills can reduce fear.
7. **Injury or loss:** Companions can face serious danger, and death or betrayal is meant to matter. Whether a lost animal can be recovered or replaced needs a final rule.
8. **Training and bonding:** Companions can gain trust and learn useful abilities; animal-handling progression can improve commands, scouting, carrying, and defense. 


### **NPC Relationships & Factions**


1. **Relationship values:** Track values such as friendship, trust, rivalry, affinity, and faction loyalty, as appropriate for each character. 
2. **Building or damaging trust:** Keep promises, help NPCs, and respect their boundaries to build trust; betrayal, dangerous actions, or feeding in front of them can damage it.
3. **Remembering choices:** NPCs should remember important events and refer to them in later dialogue, quests, and relationship stages. 
4. **Faction responses:** Factions can adjust patrols, trade, territory, quests, raids, and hostility in response to Phoenix’s actions and reputation. 
5. **Territory:** Factions can own territory and change their presence over time. The exact rules for expansion, retreat, or takeover still need defining.
6. **Quests and trade:** Reputation affects which quests, dialogue, goods, prices, and camps are available. Extreme hostility can lock Phoenix out of entire encampments. 
7. **Allies, enemies, and companions:** NPCs can become allies, rivals, enemies, merchants, or companions depending on relationship and story choices.
8. **Losing a major NPC or faction:** The loss should change relationships, available services, and possibly story or ending options. The exact consequences should be set for each major character and faction.


### **Quests, Progression & Endings**


1. **Quest structure:** Main quests advance the outbreak story; side quests develop characters and factions; optional discoveries provide lore, resources, and recipes. 
2. **Finding quests:** Players discover quests through NPCs, factions, locations, memory triggers, and story clues. A journal records objectives and discoveries. 
3. **Quest rewards:** Rewards can include resources, recipes, skills, allies, reputation, information, and new story options—not just equipment.
4. **Choices and later quests:** Important choices become story flags that alter dialogue, relationships, faction behavior, and later mission options.
5. **Quest failure:** The rules aren’t fully specified. A good default is to keep main-story quests available while allowing some optional quests to change or expire when the world changes.
6. **Endings:** Base endings on how Phoenix handles her identity and bloodlust, which factions and characters she supports, and what she does with ChroniX research. The final ending list should be consolidated from the different drafts.
7. **Progression:** Seven proposed skill areas—Stealth, Combat, Herbalism/Cannabis, Blood Alchemy, Animal Handling, Crafting, and Social—support different builds and playstyles. 
8. **New Game+:** Players may choose to carry over selected mutations, recipes, cosmetics, map knowledge, safehouses, and lore; story flags, inventory, meters, and world simulation reset. 


### **World Simulation, Weather & Events**


1. **World changes:** Crops, animals, and regional pressure can persist over time. How much simulation continues while the player is away still needs to be defined. 
2. **Day and night:** Time affects visibility, enemy aggression, human patrols, animal activity, resources, stealth, weather, and settlement activity. 
3. **Weather and seasons:** Rain, fog, storms, heat, cold, and seasonal changes can affect movement, sound, visibility, disguise, plant growth, resources, and NPC behavior. 
4. **Wildlife and plants:** Crops grow through stages and wildlife can move or become more or less active; exact population and resource-replenishment rates remain to be determined.
5. **Unprompted events:** The world can generate zombie hordes, survivor attacks, faction raids, weather events, and other encounters. 
6. **Regional responses:** Factions and threats can react to player actions and regional pressure by changing patrols, territory, or hostility.
7. **Temporary versus permanent changes:** Weather and encounters are temporary; claimed safehouses, built structures, key faction changes, and major story decisions can persist. Exact persistence rules should be documented per system.
8. **Showing world changes:** Use map updates, NPC dialogue, visible changes to locations, and journal notices so players can understand what changed when they return.


### **Economy & Trading**


1. **Currency or barter:** The current proposal is barter plus reputation, with no universal currency. 
2. **Faction values:** Human markets value water, medicine, ammunition, tools, information, and quality cannabis; conscious undead value blood, extracts, mutation knowledge, and rare recipes. 
3. **Reputation and prices:** Reputation affects prices, stock, dialogue, and access to trading locations. Visible mutations and evidence of feeding can also influence how human traders treat Phoenix.
4. **Scarcity:** Common materials should be renewable or findable in multiple regions; rare blood, research, and specialized goods should remain valuable. Exact stock and restock rates need balancing.
5. **Information and services:** Players can trade information such as map knowledge, lab secrets, and the locations of other subjects. Services can be represented through quests, repairs, or access to safe locations. 
6. **Selling crafted goods:** Let players barter useful crafted tools, clothing, extracts, and supplies where a faction or merchant values them.
7. **Faction stock:** Factions can gain or lose supplies through trade, raids, and world events; exact restocking rules remain to be defined.
8. **Rewarding playstyles:** Make different approaches economically useful: growers can trade cannabis and hemp goods, explorers can trade information, and blood alchemists can exchange rare samples or recipes.

### **UI, Accessibility & Saving**


1. **HUD:** Keep Bloodlust, Decay, disguise strength, and quick-use slots visible. Show stamina, Sanity, noise, and enemy detection cues only when relevant. 
2. **Menus and quick access:** Provide inventory, crafting, map, journal, character, relationship, and mutation screens, plus a hotbar or radial wheel for frequently used items.
3. **Teaching systems:** Introduce mechanics gradually through the opening sequence: movement, stealth, combat, gathering, crafting, and cannabis use.
4. **Adjustable settings:** Include control remapping, text scaling, colorblind modes, screen-reader support, and adjustable difficulty. Add volume settings and captions for key audio cues; captions aren’t explicitly specified in the notes. 
5. **Combat and stealth accessibility:** Offer timing and aiming assists, plus clear visual indicators for enemy detection and noise so important information doesn’t depend on hearing alone.
6. **Save slots:** Support multiple manual saves, autosave, and quicksave. The exact number of manual slots is not specified. 
7. **What saves preserve:** Save character state, inventory, skills, quests, relationships, discovered recipes, world seed, claimed bases, crops, and other persistent changes.
8. **Irreversible choices:** Before a choice can permanently damage a relationship, change a faction outcome, or lock a story route, show a clear warning and confirmation.


### **Platform, Prototype & Testing**


1. **First-release platform:** The notes list PC, browser, and Android as possibilities, but also specify different engines and platforms in different drafts. Choose one launch target first; I’d avoid committing to all three until the prototype runs well. 
2. **Controls:** Desktop plans include keyboard and mouse; mobile plans include a virtual joystick and touch buttons. Controller support is a useful option to decide, but isn’t specified yet. 
3. **Prototype features:** The proposed vertical slice includes one region, movement and climbing, survival meters, basic cannabis use, a few blood mixes, the Multi-Stick, basic disguise, two or three enemy types, crafting, shelter, inventory, saving, the opening sequence, and a day/night cycle. 
4. **Features to postpone:** Large-scale world expansion, full faction simulation, many animal species, advanced mutations, multiple endings, extensive audio, and mod support can follow once the core loop works.
5. **Performance targets:** Set a measurable frame-rate and loading target for the chosen launch device; exact numbers aren’t specified. Test world streaming, NPC counts, and memory use on minimum supported hardware.
6. **Bug reporting:** Have testers submit the build/platform, steps to reproduce, expected and actual result, and optional screenshots or clips. Tag bugs by severity and whether they block progress.
7. **Testing the core loop:** Check whether new players understand their objective and meters, complete the gather–craft–survive loop, recognize detection, and find the tension engaging rather than confusing.
8. **Release readiness:** Require stable saves, tested controls, completed core content, accessibility checks, resolved blocking bugs, and platform/content-rating compliance before release.

1. **Species:** Dogs, cats, wolves, rabbits, rats, deer, and birds are proposed; other tameable species are possible. Finalize a smaller launch roster first.
2. **Taming:** Approach carefully, offer food, and build trust through repeated interactions. Animal-handling skills can speed this up.
3. **Roles:** Assign Guard, Scout, Hauler, Companion, or Mount roles, with abilities such as tracking, finding supplies, scouting, and carrying resources. 
4. **Commands:** Provide follow, stay, explore, and return commands, plus role-specific orders as needed. 
5. **Needs:** Track animal health, hunger, loyalty, and shelter; players can feed, train, and house them.
6. **Bloodlust and mutations:** Animals can respond to Phoenix’s Bloodlust, disguise, and mutations according to species and temperament. Exact response rules still need to be set.
7. **Injury or loss:** Companions can be endangered, and death or betrayal is meant to carry weight. Decide whether they can be recovered or replaced.
8. **Training and bonding:** Bonding and Animal Handling progression can unlock commands, scouting, carrying, and defensive abilities. 


### **NPC Relationships & Factions**


1. **Values:** Track trust, affinity, rivalry, friendship, and faction loyalty where relevant. 
2. **Trust and betrayal:** Helping characters, keeping promises, and respecting boundaries can build trust; feeding, betrayal, or endangering others can damage it.
3. **NPC memory:** Major events should be remembered and reflected in later dialogue, quests, and relationship states. 
4. **Faction response:** Factions can change patrols, trade, territory, quests, raids, and hostility according to Phoenix’s actions and reputation. 
5. **Territory:** Factions can gain or lose territory, but exact expansion and takeover rules remain to be designed.
6. **Quest and trade effects:** Reputation can unlock or restrict quests, dialogue, camps, goods, and prices. 
7. **NPC outcomes:** NPCs can become allies, enemies, merchants, or companions based on choices and relationships.
8. **Losing a major character or faction:** Loss should affect available support, services, story branches, or endings. Define consequences for each major NPC and faction rather than using one generic penalty.


I’ll read the attached quest and progression questions, then answer every section included.


The attachment contains five sections, including **Game Foundations & Vision**. Here are answers to all of them; I’ve noted where the design documents conflict or leave a decision open.


### **Quests, Progression & Endings**


1. **Organization:** Main quests advance the outbreak story; side quests develop characters and factions; optional tasks reward exploration with clues, resources, and recipes. 
2. **Discovery:** Players find quests through NPCs, factions, locations, memories, and research clues. Add discoveries to the journal and map.
3. **Rewards:** Quests can provide skills, recipes, allies, reputation, information, resources, and new story options—not just equipment.
4. **Choice effects:** Record major decisions as story flags that influence later dialogue, relationships, faction behavior, and quest availability.
5. **Failure:** Keep main-story quests available when possible; optional quests can change as the world changes. Clearly signal when an action will close off a side quest.
6. **Endings:** Base endings on Phoenix’s identity choices, bloodlust, faction loyalties, relationships, and what she does with ChroniX research. The notes contain different proposed ending lists, so consolidate them into one final roster.
7. **Playstyles:** Seven proposed skill areas—Stealth, Combat, Herbalism/Cannabis, Blood Alchemy, Animal Handling, Crafting, and Social—support different character builds. 
8. **New Game+:** Let players choose whether to carry over selected mutations, recipes, cosmetics, map knowledge, safehouses, and lore. Reset story flags, inventory, meters, and world simulation. 


### **World Simulation, Weather & Events**


1. **Changes while away:** Crops, animals, and regional pressure can persist over time. Decide how much simulation continues while an area is unloaded.
2. **Day and night:** Time affects visibility, enemy aggression, patrols, animal activity, resource availability, stealth, weather, and settlement activity. 
3. **Weather and seasons:** Weather can affect sound, visibility, movement, disguise, plant growth, resources, and NPC behavior; seasonal changes can alter those conditions further. 
4. **Wildlife and crops:** Plants grow and animals can change local activity or population. Exact growth, migration, and replenishment rates still need to be balanced.
5. **Events:** Generate encounters such as zombie hordes, survivor attacks, faction raids, and environmental events. Use authored versions for important story beats and generated versions for variety. 
6. **Faction and threat response:** Let factions react to player actions and local pressure by changing patrols, territory, trade, or hostility.
7. **Temporary and permanent changes:** Weather and many encounters are temporary; claimed safehouses, built structures, and major story or faction changes can persist.
8. **Showing changes:** Use visible changes to locations, NPC dialogue, map updates, and journal notices so players understand what happened while they were away.


### **Economy & Trading**


1. **Currency:** The proposed economy uses barter and reputation rather than universal currency. 
2. **Faction values:** Human markets value clean water, medicine, ammunition, tools, information, and quality cannabis. The conscious-undead network values blood, extracts, mutation knowledge, and rare recipes. 
3. **Reputation and prices:** Reputation changes prices, stock, dialogue, and access to trading locations. Human traders may also react to visible mutations or evidence of feeding.
4. **Scarcity:** Make common supplies renewable or available across multiple regions; keep rare blood, research, and specialized goods valuable. Exact supply levels need balancing.
5. **Information and services:** Allow trades involving information such as map knowledge, lab secrets, and the locations of other subjects. Services can include repairs, safe passage, or access to facilities.
6. **Crafted goods:** Let players barter useful tools, clothing, cannabis products, and other crafted supplies.
7. **Faction supplies:** Factions can gain or lose resources through trade, raids, and world events. Their restocking rules still need to be defined.
8. **Different playstyles:** Make growing, hunting, crafting, exploring, and social play each produce goods or information that some faction values.


### **UI, Accessibility & Saving**


1. **HUD:** Keep Bloodlust, Decay, disguise strength, and quick-use slots visible; show stamina, sanity, noise, and detection indicators contextually. 
2. **Menus and quick access:** Provide inventory, crafting, map, journal, character, relationship, and mutation screens, plus a hotbar or radial wheel for frequently used items.
3. **Teaching systems:** Use the opening sequence to teach movement and interaction, stealth, combat, gathering, crafting, and cannabis use one step at a time.
4. **Adjustable settings:** Include control remapping, text scaling, colorblind modes, screen-reader support, and adjustable difficulty. Add audio sliders and captions for key sounds; captions aren’t specified in the notes yet. 
5. **Combat and stealth accessibility:** Offer timing, aiming, and detection assists, with visual cues for important audio information.
6. **Save slots:** Support several manual saves, autosave, and quicksave. The exact manual-slot count remains open. 
7. **What saves preserve:** Save inventory, skills, quests, relationships, discovered recipes, world seed, claimed safehouses, crops, and other persistent world state.
8. **Irreversible choices:** Warn and ask for confirmation before choices that permanently damage relationships, change faction outcomes, or lock story routes.


### **Platform, Prototype & Testing**


1. **First platform:** The notes list PC, browser, and Android as possibilities, but don’t settle on a single launch platform or engine. Choose one first; don’t promise support for all until it’s tested. 
2. **Controls:** Plan keyboard and mouse for desktop and a virtual joystick with touch buttons for mobile. Controller support still needs a decision. 
3. **First prototype:** Build one region with movement, climbing, core survival meters, basic cannabis use, a few blood mixes, Multi-Stick uses, disguise, two or three enemy types, crafting, shelter, inventory, saving, the opening, and day/night. 
4. **Later features:** Defer large-scale world expansion, numerous factions and animals, advanced mutations, multiple endings, full audio, and mod support until the core loop works.
5. **Performance target:** Set a frame-rate and load-time target for the chosen minimum device; exact numbers aren’t specified. Test world streaming, NPC counts, and memory use on that device.
6. **Bug reporting:** Ask testers to provide build and device information, steps to reproduce, expected and actual results, and optional screenshots or clips. Prioritize bugs by severity.
7. **Core-loop feedback:** Check whether players understand their objective and meters, complete the gather–craft–survive loop without outside help, recognize detection, and find the tension engaging rather than confusing.
8. **Release readiness:** Require stable saves, tested controls, complete core content, accessibility checks, no blocking bugs, and platform and age-rating compliance.


### **Game Foundations & Vision**


1. **Working title:** The most consistent current title is **HIGH & UNDEAD: NATURAL SURVIVAL**, but other drafts use different titles. Pick one official title before creating public-facing materials. 
2. **One-sentence description:** A 2D open-world survival game where Phoenix, a conscious zombie, gathers, crafts, manages bloodlust, and disguises herself to survive among humans and the undead while uncovering the ChroniX outbreak.
3. **Genre:** 2D top-down open-world survival-crafting, stealth, RPG, and narrative sandbox.
4. **Platforms and format:** The drafts mention PC, browser, and Android but conflict on engine and launch target. Choose one for the first release; the format isn’t settled yet. 
5. **Audience and age:** The concept targets adult players and includes horror, gore, cannabis-related fictional gameplay, and optional mature relationships. Set a target rating and content warnings before release.
6. **Player role:** The player is Phoenix, a newly turned zombie who retains her human consciousness.
7. **Design pillars:** **Survive, disguise, and evolve**—supported by exploration, crafting, bloodlust management, and memory recovery.
8. **Distinctive hook:** The player is the zombie, must blend into both human and undead groups, and evolves through blood mixing while cannabis is integrated into survival and crafting.
9. **Tone and emotion:** Dark humor, body horror, melancholy, moments of tenderness, and hard-won clarity. 
10. **Out of scope for the first release:** An unlimited world, every proposed feature, and systems that aren’t necessary to prove the core loop. Build the one-region playable slice first, then expand.


## **2. Premise, Setting & Player Promise**


1. **World at the beginning:** The game opens during the first chaotic phase of the outbreak—not years later. The city still has apartments, stores, transit, and emergency services, but routines are collapsing: alarms and sirens compete, traffic is jammed, broadcasts contradict each other, and people are fleeing or barricading themselves.


2. **Cause of the outbreak:** ChroniX’s experimental Euphoria strain was contaminated with a viral agent, and the outbreak spread during a company event. The public initially knows only that people are becoming violent and ill; the corporate connection and Phoenix’s involvement are revealed gradually through alerts, research, records, and memories. 


3. **Phoenix’s immediate situation:** Phoenix wakes in her apartment with a gap in her memory and assumes it’s an ordinary workday. She is newly turned, but her conscious mind hasn’t yet accepted what her body has become. Her morning routine gives her—and the player—time to notice that something is wrong before the city confirms it.


4. **Player promise:** Play as a thinking zombie, not a human survivor. Survive by gathering, crafting, hiding, adapting, and deciding how much of Phoenix’s humanity she wants to keep.


5. **Central theme:** **What remains of a person when their mind is still present, but their body and instincts have changed?** The game keeps asking whether Phoenix can control her hunger, whether she should seek restoration, and whether humans and undead can coexist.


6. **First goal:** At first, Phoenix’s goal is mundane: get ready and go to work. After she steps outside, that goal becomes immediate survival—and finding out why the city has changed and why her own body feels wrong.


7. **How the goal changes:** Survive the immediate outbreak, understand her transformation, uncover ChroniX’s role, then decide whether to pursue a cure, accept her undead nature, or help create coexistence.


8. **Familiar versus strange:** The apartment, shower, breakfast, work clothes, phone, and commute should feel ordinary. The wrong temperature of her skin, absence of a normal heartbeat, strange appetite, distorted senses, zombies ignoring her, and humans recoiling from her should feel increasingly impossible.


9. **Themes and motifs:** Routine against chaos; mirrors and identity; hunger versus self-control; blood and disguise; corporate memory and guilt; gardens and regrowth. Repeated visual details—an alarm that keeps going off, a mirror, a work badge, and the same city street before and after the outbreak—can tie the opening to later memories.


10. **What players should remember:** An almost normal morning that gradually turns into the realization that Phoenix is a zombie—and the unusual survival fantasy of learning to pass among both humans and the undead.


## **3. Opening, Prologue & First Hour**


1. **First image:** A phone alarm vibrates on a bedside table in a dim apartment. The screen reads **7:12 AM**. A work reminder appears beneath several missed emergency alerts from overnight, but Phoenix is too groggy to understand their significance.


2. **Where Phoenix begins—and the detailed opening:**


   - **Waking up:** Phoenix silences the alarm. The room looks lived-in: work clothes laid out, a bag near the door, dishes from the night before, and a ChroniX badge or work item among her things. The player learns movement and simple interaction by sitting up, checking the room, and getting ready.
   - **The morning routine:** She heads to the bathroom, turns on the shower, and gets ready for work. Keep it familiar and quiet, but introduce small wrong details: the water feels cold even when it’s hot, she doesn’t feel fully awake, and everyday sounds—the pipes, the refrigerator, a neighbor moving next door—seem unusually loud.
   - **The mirror:** After the shower, she wipes steam from the mirror. At first she just looks tired. Then she notices her skin is unusually pale, her eyes look wrong, and a dark mark or wound is partly hidden by her hair or collar. Her reflection may seem subtly unfamiliar, but avoid explaining the cause yet. Phoenix dismisses it as illness, exhaustion, or bad lighting.
   - **Getting dressed and breakfast:** She chooses work clothes, gathers her bag and keys, and attempts breakfast. Food has little taste or feels unpleasant; coffee smells familiar but doesn’t wake her up. A phone alert interrupts: emergency services are reporting an incident nearby, while a ChroniX message says employees should await instructions. Phoenix assumes the alerts are about a separate crisis.
   - **Clues from the previous night:** Messages and a calendar reminder hint that something happened at a ChroniX event. A coworker’s unfinished message—“Don’t come in. Something’s wrong with the—”—is cut off. The player can inspect these clues, but none should fully explain the outbreak.
   - **Leaving the apartment:** In the hallway, the building feels too quiet. A door is ajar; an elevator is stopped; there’s a distant thud or an indistinct call for help. Phoenix can take the stairs, learn basic movement and interaction, and encounter the first signs that the danger is close.
   - **Stepping outside:** The street turns the ordinary morning into an emergency. Sirens, abandoned cars, an alarm still sounding, people running, and infected moving through the crowd create a sharp contrast with the apartment. A zombie passes Phoenix without attacking. A frightened human sees her, screams, and runs.
   - **First loss of control:** A wounded person or sudden burst of blood triggers Phoenix’s hunger. The player feels the bloodlust rise and must choose whether to back away, hide, or approach. This introduces her condition as a playable problem without forcing the player to harm someone.
   - **The first escape:** A nearby attack or crowd surge forces Phoenix to escape the street. She can use an alley, a stairwell, or a building entrance to break line of sight. The sequence teaches sneaking, sound, and hiding through the situation rather than a detached tutorial.


3. **When control begins:** Give the player control shortly after the alarm, once Phoenix is awake. Let them explore the apartment and perform parts of the routine before the first outside danger.


4. **What the opening establishes:** Phoenix thinks she is human and trying to get to work, but her body, senses, and the infected world contradict that assumption. The first clear evidence is that zombies don’t attack her while humans react as if she is one of them.


5. **First objective:** Start with **Get ready for work**. Once the player sees the street, change it to **Find somewhere safe**; after reaching shelter, the next goal becomes **Find out what happened at ChroniX**.


6. **Systems taught:** Apartment: movement, interaction, inspection, and inventory. Hallway: sound and attention. Street: stealth, suspicion, and bloodlust. First safe location: gathering, basic crafting, and checking Phoenix’s survival meters.


7. **First danger:** The first danger is the public outbreak spilling into Phoenix’s immediate neighborhood. The first gameplay threat should be detection and a bloodlust spike—not a mandatory combat encounter.


8. **Important item or clue:** Phoenix’s phone and ChroniX badge are key opening props. The phone provides emergency alerts and a coworker’s interrupted message; the badge establishes her connection to ChroniX and can later unlock or identify restricted locations.


9. **First moment of safety:** Treat Phoenix’s apartment as the **first provisional safehouse**. After escaping the street, she can return, lock or barricade the door, and take a breath. Later, she can choose to secure it properly or move elsewhere.


10. **End of the opening section:** End the first major section with a new warning: a recognizable coworker’s voice or message says ChroniX has sealed its campus and is searching for a missing employee or test subject. Phoenix checks her badge or a recovered record and realizes the mystery may be about her. That gives the player a clear next objective—reach the ChroniX perimeter—without revealing the full truth too soon.


1. **What first made you want to create this game?**  
   The concept seems to start with turning the usual zombie-survival idea around: instead of playing a human fighting zombies, the player is a zombie trying to survive. The unusual combination of zombie survival, cannabis, crafting, and mature content makes that perspective your own. 


2. **What moment or image made the idea exciting?**  
   A normal workday morning slowly feeling wrong: Phoenix showers, dresses, and tries to eat breakfast, notices something strange in the mirror, then steps outside and discovers that the city has become an outbreak zone—and that she is one of the zombies. That contrast between routine and horror is a strong opening image.


3. **What experience do you wish existing games gave you?**  
   The chance to experience survival from a conscious zombie’s point of view: managing bloodlust, avoiding human detection, blending in with zombies, and deciding how to respond to both groups. The cannabis system should be part of that survival experience, not just decoration.


4. **What should players get to do that they usually can’t?**  
   Grow and use cannabis as an in-game survival system: make products such as joints, pipes, papers, or bongs, then use them to help manage Phoenix’s bloodlust. Those items should be fictional gameplay tools with clear effects and limits.


5. **What should an ordinary minute of play feel like?**  
   Quiet but uneasy. The player might scavenge, manage supplies, disguise Phoenix, or explore—while listening for danger and deciding whether to risk a fight or take another route.


6. **What should major success, loss, or discovery feel like?**  
   Success should bring relief and a sense that the player adapted or made a smart choice. Loss should feel consequential, especially when it affects a companion or relationship. A major discovery should change how the player understands Phoenix, ChroniX, or the outbreak.


7. **What is the one experience the game must deliver?**  
   The experience of being a thinking zombie who must survive between human and undead worlds while deciding what she wants to become.


8. **Which parts are non-negotiable?**  
   The cannabis system, the NSFW/mature-content system, and the protagonist being a zombie. These should remain connected to the overall game rather than feel like separate add-ons.


9. **Which parts can change?**  
   The exact map size, platform, engine, number of regions, combat balance, crafting recipes, faction details, and whether co-op is added can change if a better or more manageable design emerges. Keep the core experience intact while adjusting the scope.


10. **What should the game not become?**  
   It shouldn’t become a conventional game where the zombie is only an enemy, a game where cannabis is just a collectible, or one where mature content replaces the survival and story. It should also avoid becoming so large in scope that its central gameplay loop never gets polished.


11. **Which works inspired it?**  
   Your notes name *Z Girls 2: Last One*, *Dawn of Zombies*, *Days After*, *The Forest*, *DayZ*, *Rust*, and *The Last of Us* as reference points for survival, crafting, tension, and post-apocalyptic settings. The goal is to draw on broad genre ideas while making the conscious-zombie perspective and integrated cannabis system distinct. 


12. **What would make it feel like the game you wanted to make?**  
   A playable game where the morning-apartment opening leads naturally into the outbreak; playing as a zombie changes how survival works; and cannabis, crafting, stealth, and mature content all support the same core experience.


**Does this capture your intent, especially the morning-to-outbreak opening and the three non-negotiables?**


I’ll read the attached scope and prototype questions, then answer each set in the context of your game.


Here’s a proposed **developer baseline** for the whole attachment. These are recommendations for turning *High & Undead* into a buildable project; I haven’t invented a budget, team size, or release date.


### **Scope & First Playable Version**


1. **Smallest fun version:** A short playable slice where Phoenix wakes in her apartment, follows her routine, sees the outbreak, escapes one street encounter, and reaches a temporary safehouse.
2. **Must-have features:** Movement and interaction, apartment-to-street opening, basic Bloodlust and disguise, one gathering/crafting loop, cannabis use, two enemy types, one safe place, and saving.
3. **Postpone:** Multiple regions, full faction simulation, large animal roster, advanced mutation trees, co-op, many endings, and extensive mature scenes. Keep the NSFW setting option in the prototype, but the content itself can be minimal.
4. **Realistic prototype size:** One small neighborhood, one main objective, one optional discovery, two or three enemy types, and around 20–30 minutes of play. Treat that as a vertical slice, not the full release.
5. **Success evidence:** Test whether new players understand the objective, notice Phoenix’s condition, complete the first survival loop, and can describe whether playing as a zombie feels meaningfully different.


The existing design notes also propose a one-region vertical slice with core survival meters, cannabis use, a few blood mixes, Multi-Stick, disguise, two or three enemies, crafting, shelter, and saving. 


### **Technology & Platforms**


1. **Engine:** I recommend Godot 4 for the 2D game and its growing systems. The notes conflict: one draft specifies GDevelop/HTML5, while another recommends Godot 4.
2. **First platform:** Choose PC first; treat browser and Android as later ports after controls and performance are proven.
3. **Minimum target:** Aim for smooth 60 FPS on modest supported PC hardware at 1080p; finalize the exact minimum spec after profiling.
4. **Online requirement:** Make the single-player game fully playable offline. Don’t require accounts, multiplayer, or a paid AI service.
5. **Technical limits:** Stream a finite world in manageable chunks. Set a tested cap for nearby NPCs, effects, and active encounters instead of promising an unlimited world. 


### **Team, Schedule & Production**


1. **Responsibilities:** Cover design, code, art, writing, audio, and testing; one person may hold multiple roles.
2. **Team model:** The notes don’t say whether this is solo or team development. Plan the prototype so a solo developer can build it, then add contributors if available.
3. **Budget and timeline:** These can’t be responsibly set without knowing the team, working hours, and funding. Estimate them only after the vertical-slice task list is sized.
4. **Dependencies:** Lock the engine and controls first; then build movement, interaction, and the apartment/street scenes; then add survival, stealth, crafting, art, and polish.
5. **If a feature takes too long:** Preserve the core zombie-survival loop and cut or simplify secondary systems rather than shipping many unfinished features.


### **Assets & Development Workflow**


1. **Asset creation:** Use a consistent 2D top-down art pipeline for characters, environments, UI, animation, and sound.
2. **Asset standards:** Define sprite sizes, animation frames, color palettes, file formats, audio levels, and naming rules before producing large asset sets.
3. **Asset rights:** Track whether each asset is original, licensed, or temporary placeholder art; keep licenses and attribution details with the project.
4. **Backups and collaboration:** Use version control and regular backups for code, art, writing, and audio.
5. **Content editing:** Store items, recipes, dialogue, quests, and effects in organized data files so changes don’t require rewriting core logic.


### **Testing & Quality**


1. **Milestone tests:** Test controls and scene transitions first, then survival meters, stealth detection, crafting, saving, and the complete opening loop.
2. **Playtesters:** Start with a small group unfamiliar with the design; collect notes on where they get confused, stuck, or disengaged.
3. **Bug tracking:** Record steps to reproduce, device/build, expected result, actual result, and severity; retest after fixes.
4. **Balance testing:** Track how often players are detected, lose control, run out of resources, or avoid combat. Adjust rates based on observed play, not assumptions.
5. **Release gate:** Require a stable build, working saves, clear controls, no blocking bugs, and complete core content before release. The notes already call for alpha testing and iteration. 


### **Accessibility, Ratings & Rights**


1. **Accessibility:** Include remappable controls, scalable text, colorblind options, screen-reader support where feasible, and combat assists. 
2. **Rating and warnings:** Target an adult rating and provide clear warnings for horror, gore, cannabis-related fictional content, and mature relationship themes.
3. **Mature-content settings:** Let players disable or limit mature content, and verify that each selected platform supports the intended presentation.
4. **Ownership:** Confirm ownership and license terms for code, art, writing, music, fonts, and third-party tools.
5. **Distribution rules:** Check each storefront’s current age-rating, content, privacy, and payment requirements before committing to launch there.


### **Business & Release Plan**


1. **Store:** For a PC-first version, choose a PC storefront and maintain a separate browser build only if it fits the technical plan.
2. **Price and model:** The notes consider a paid game with optional cosmetics or expansions and reject pay-to-win or paywalling core survival systems. Exact pricing is still open. 
3. **Launch format:** Prove the core loop in a prototype first; decide between a demo, Early Access, or full release after scope and production capacity are clearer.
4. **Store materials:** Prepare a clear description, screenshots, trailer, feature list, content warnings, and supported-platform details.
5. **Communicating scope:** Clearly distinguish what is playable at launch from ideas planned for later, so players aren’t promised unfinished systems.


### **Post-Launch Support**


1. **Support owner:** Assign a person or team to triage bug reports and player questions.
2. **Update cadence:** Set a realistic patch schedule based on team capacity; don’t promise frequent updates before knowing the workload.
3. **Save safety:** Version saves and test migrations before updates; back up saves where the platform allows it.
4. **Expansions and mods:** Consider new regions, chapters, and mod support after the core game is stable. These are proposed future options, not commitments.
5. **If support ends:** Communicate clearly, preserve the last stable version where possible, and avoid leaving saves dependent on unavailable online services.


### **Data, Privacy & Security**


1. **Data collection:** Collect only what the game needs; disclose optional crash reports or analytics and ask for consent where required.
2. **Offline access:** Keep the core game playable without an account or external service.
3. **Save protection:** Use versioned saves, backups, and clear recovery behavior if a save is damaged.
4. **Third-party records:** Maintain a list of external tools, libraries, services, and licenses.
5. **Privacy information:** Provide a plain-language privacy notice if any personal data, analytics, or online services are used.


### **Game Flow**


1. **Launch screen:** Show the title, content settings, accessibility options, and clear paths to start or continue.
2. **Start to play:** Move from title screen to character creation, then into the apartment-morning prologue.
3. **Session flow:** Let players pause, save where allowed, resume, return to the title, or load another save.
4. **After the story:** Offer New Game+, challenge modifiers, and continued exploration only if they can be supported without undermining the ending.


### **Camera & Controls**


1. **Camera:** Use a top-down camera for exploration and combat, with limited zoom or framing changes for important scenes.
2. **Movement and actions:** Use directional movement, contextual interaction, and quick inputs for attacks and survival items.
3. **Combat targeting:** Use facing-direction attacks for melee and cursor or aim controls for ranged options, with lock-on assist available.
4. **Control schemes:** Support keyboard and mouse first; add controller support for PC, and touch controls only if mobile becomes a launch target. The notes already propose keyboard and touch controls. 


### **Rules & Outcomes**


1. **Ordinary objective:** Survive, gather, craft, manage bloodlust and disguise, and pursue the next clue or objective.
2. **Defeat or loss of control:** Standard defeat returns the player to a save or safehouse rest; losing control can cause relationship and faction consequences. Optional permadeath can be a separate mode. 
3. **Mission failure:** Let optional quests fail or change when the world changes, but keep major story routes understandable and avoid invisible fail conditions.
4. **Objective completion:** Use a journal, map markers, dialogue, and clear completion feedback to show when an objective is done or an ending is reached.


### **Scenes & Build Requirements**


1. **First-build scenes:** Title/menu, character creation, apartment, apartment building/hallway, nearby street, temporary safehouse, inventory/crafting, and a basic defeat/save flow.
2. **Required content:** Phoenix, a few civilians, two or three enemy types, interactable household items, gathering points, one important clue, and a small neighborhood environment.
3. **Transitions:** Keep the apartment, hallway, street, and nearby interiors connected through short, readable transitions; stream areas where feasible.
4. **Build first:** Create the apartment-to-street opening first. It tests the protagonist, tone, controls, first reveal, and the key survival loop in one compact slice.


### **Player Feedback & Interface**


1. **Condition:** Keep Bloodlust, Decay, disguise strength, and quick-use slots visible; show stamina, sanity, and suspicion cues when relevant. 
2. **Interactable objects:** Use contextual prompts and distinct visual states for usable, gatherable, and examinable objects.
3. **Danger cues:** Give enemies readable animations and audio/visual signals when they notice, investigate, or pursue Phoenix.
4. **Teaching controls:** Teach through short contextual prompts and safe practice moments rather than pausing the game for long tutorials.


### **Saving & World State**


1. **Save locations:** Support manual saves, autosave, and quicksave; safehouses can also serve as reliable rest/save points. 
2. **Persistent state:** Save inventory, relationships, quest choices, recipes, buildings, crops, world seed, and important regional/faction changes.
3. **After defeat or story choices:** Loading a save restores the state from that point; warn players before choices that permanently alter relationships or routes.
4. **Preventing corruption:** Use versioned saves, backups, error handling, and never silently overwrite an existing save. 


### **First Playable Build**


1. **Smallest demonstration:** One continuous sequence from waking in the apartment to reaching a temporary safehouse after the first street encounter.
2. **Must work, not just appear:** Movement, interaction, basic stealth, bloodlust feedback, one gathering/crafting action, cannabis use, an enemy detection response, and saving.
3. **Tester’s task:** Start without instructions, discover the immediate objective, leave the apartment, respond to the street threat, reach safety, and explain what Phoenix is and what she needs to do next.
4. **Change the concept if:** Players don’t understand the zombie perspective, the opening reveal doesn’t land, or the core gather–hide–survive loop feels less interesting than a conventional combat game.
